0,2026-08-09,"Riders and teams frequently face financial hardship in the professional peloton
Team cars shuttle from side to side on damp roads through picturesque Yorkshire villages as the race convoy tries to keep up with a marauding peloton.
Under-pressure sporting directors at the 2025 Tour of Britain Women are shouting tactics over the radio with one hand while steering their vehicles with the other.
Then, a concertina, in which Hess Cycling's team car slams on the brakes and hits the back of another team's vehicle, making that uneasy sound of one carâ¯colliding withâ¯another.
It was a fitting metaphor for a team who shot forward too quickly and paid the price.
Within two months of that incident, the Hess team who had aimed so high when launching two years earlier had collapsed.
""We got promised the world -â¯aâ¯big elaborate dream,"" says a former Hess rider, who does not want to beâ¯identified through fear of being judged as a troublemaker in the peloton.â¯
""Itâ¯seems like it was created for one person,â¯not for the benefit of riders.â¯You'veâ¯got to wonder why someone puts their name on the jersey.""
Gone were the aims to have one of the biggest budgets on the ever-growing UCI Women's World Tour, putting Britain back on the cycling map with a world-beating team.
Replaced by riders and team members who felt let down and remain - one year after the team's demise - out of pocket. Quite a lot out of pocket. 
One team supplier we spoke to is owed more than Â£50,000 - confirmed in documents seen by the BBC.
With one year to go until the Tour de France and Tour de France Femmes roll into the UK to inspire another generation of cyclists, BBC Sport asks: Why is it so hard to run a cycling team?
The Hess team's demise centres around the loss of their title sponsor after they pulled out in response to media coverage of a civil claim against ""one of the team's directors"" over a separate business venture.
But were the team's hands tied from the very beginning by a financial model very different to sports such as football and Formula 1?
Back in October 2023, in a Manchester coffee house, Swiss team co-owner Rolf Hess passionately laid out plans for a British team to win the biggest races in women's cycling, including the Tour de France Femmes.
There would be good money, and a fresh, athlete-led approach to finding sponsors and generating ""riders as brands"" to amplify both rider and team - all led by bosses with an eye for detail and heart in the project.
British talent would be invested in, races would be won and a new world order - in a sport currently dominated by Dutch teams and riders - achieved.
Team Sky made it look easy a decade prior in men's cycling. How hard could it be?
""Our first-year budget is 750,000 euros, with no sponsor at all,""â¯said Hessâ¯at the time.
""Thisâ¯is entrepreneurship - we do it ourselves.â¯Weâ¯didn'tâ¯buy a team - we are building a team."" 
Heâ¯added. ""We feel we need to take care of our riders and invite them to take up studies while they race and create their own personalised consumer product.""
With the first instalment of 750,000 euros already pledged, the team's budget was intended to be five times that when they made the World Tour by 2025.
The team lost a title sponsor mid-season
""We were lucky if we even saw half a million [as a team], I'd say,"" says the former rider. 
""There was even no money at team camps and other stuff theyâ¯didn'tâ¯pay for. There were big bills.
""I know there were other riders still owed money - a lot of it.""
It all began with some modest results, but a 15thâ¯place at the 2025â¯Women's Tour of Britainâ¯was about as good as it got in the bigger races, and the team never made it above 'continental' level, which is the third tier of women's road cycling.
The rider says: ""There were reasonable money issuesâ¯fromâ¯theâ¯start where ridersâ¯wereâ¯paid late orâ¯wouldn'tâ¯getâ¯reimbursements.â¯
""Itâ¯pushes you into [bad] situations.""
And itâ¯wasn'tâ¯just the riders - business partners who supplied the team with equipment are owed a lot of money.
One mechanic - who asked to remain anonymous through fear of having any future employment jeopardised - claims to have had to find hire vehicles a day before a race upon landing at an airport to discover nothing had been arranged. 
As well as trying to prepare bikes and equipment - while finding his own way to pay for it.
Money was promised to be settled at various stated points since the team's demise in August last year, but the latest statement from Hessâ¯saysâ¯""negotiations regarding funding is ongoing despite headwinds and a difficult market situation"".
Some have been waiting to be paid forâ¯aâ¯year.
In a statement Hess said: ""Unfortunately,â¯[our]â¯sponsor withdrew from the partnership at the beginning of 2025. 
""Hess Sports Group continued to fund operations, contributing more than Â£200,000 over the course of summer 2025, while working to enable the team to compete under a financially sustainable model. 
""This shortfall in funding had a material impact on ourâ¯operations, butâ¯allowed our athletes to compete while we worked on securing the team's future.
""Hess Sports Group remains committed to repaying all its creditors.""
At the outset, Hess the man was full of passion for the project, and praise for the athletes.
But there were plenty of other distractions given the Hess Group includes oil and gas producerâ¯Carraigmore, water purificationâ¯companiesâ¯and make-up businesses.
The civil claim initiated against a group which includes the director of the cycling team was in Spain, over accusations of misleading investors for the delayed floating of a water company on the stock market. The allegations are denied.
Norbert Stocker, a member of the cycling team's management, told BBC Sport the loss of a title sponsor occurred after the sponsor was spooked over the allegations relating to the water company.
""To influence the commercial negotiations with equity investors in a separate company, the cycling team was targeted to exert pressure, even though it is wholly unrelated,"" he said.
""Although all the claims are unfounded, media speculation destabilised the relationship with the cycling team's core external sponsor.""
Is women's cycling broken? Or, more to the point, is cycling broken? 
Unlike most other sports, teams do not see a penny of TV rights money - that goes to race organisers, such as ASO, who bring fans the Tour de France, among many other races.
This puts huge financial pressure on teams from the start, as they must find several sponsors for their income, and cover ever more costly ways to gain an advantage against rivals in performance and technology. Let alone consider paying a team of more than 30 riders and several staff.
If a team loses their sponsor, then their funding has gone. There's virtually nothing else to fall back on.
In the past two years alone about 10 British-based men's or women's 'continental' teams have folded.
Many in the sport believe ASO's market dominance holds the key to a more financially stable future in road cycling, by being part of wider negotiations with teams to allow them a slice of global TV rights revenue which would help the sport grow commercially to benefit all parties.
The 'one cycling' idea, as it has been known for some years, has been pushed by many teams and their leaders, but has never come to fruition, partly thanks to a lack of interest from the biggest earners in the sport.
In January this year cycling's world governing body the UCI launched a consultation period for men's and women's cycling on ""key topics, including the economic model, the calendar and participation rules, fan engagement, safety and the credibility of sporting results"".
Without TV rights money, teams struggle in the longer term. The winner of the Tour de France Femmes receives a tenth of that of the men's race
Nevertheless, riders and other team associates believe Hess should have done a better job of looking after those who worked for it, despite a difficult working landscape.
Some of the money owed was also paid back by the team through a bank guarantee which teams are obliged to supply to British Cycling upon registration.
""Riders have too high expectations,"" says Hess sporting directorâ¯Pirminâ¯Lang, a former rider on the men's World Tour.
""It'sâ¯more about the fact that women's cycling has developed too quickly. Teams try to setâ¯up, butâ¯can'tâ¯keep up. Mostly of a financial nature.
""The topâ¯[World Tour]â¯is gettingâ¯bigger and better, but theâ¯[development]â¯area is missing. And that is the base of the long-term structure of cycling. Not just the pro sport, also the economy.""
The anonymous former Hess rider concludes: ""I'll stand upâ¯for myself and actually say things and maybe that doesn't help my career in the long run,â¯but I've worked a normal job and a normalâ¯workplace and I understand yourâ¯rights as an employee...
""I thinkâ¯theâ¯thingâ¯thatâ¯wasâ¯reallyâ¯getting to me in the teamâ¯wasâ¯there'sâ¯nothingâ¯that protects you, so you keep quiet.
""Sometimes girls paidâ¯[to getâ¯to races] - andâ¯that'sâ¯becauseâ¯they'reâ¯scaredâ¯for their career,â¯becauseâ¯what if this is their breakthrough race? And it all gets better after that?
""You'reâ¯always 'looking for the rainbow'...â¯so theâ¯team holds all the power over you because you're desperate and want the dream.
""Theâ¯team had aâ¯duty to us asâ¯ridersâ¯and theyâ¯â¯failed.""

1,2026-08-09,"Douglas Craig's heart was racing, and his adrenaline was pumping.
Just a few weeks after finishing his training, Craig and his colleague had to belay a family down an outdoor ropes course during an unexpected lightning storm.
Craig's summer is looking a lot different from his decades as a media executive; he now spends time rescuing people stuck in trees and wearing a harness at an aerial adventure park in Colorado.
After being laid off in June 2025, Craig looked for media roles. However, the job cut ultimately became the catalyst for a career pivot, albeit one that came with a pay cut. He worked as a ski instructor in the winter, which he's planning to do again, and as an instructor at a science camp in the spring.
""I didn't feel like I was starting over; I just kind of feel like I was shifting to something that just brought more happiness and joy,"" he said.
Craig is among the Americans navigating a new phase of career reinvention, one driven less by opportunity than by a need to adapt. AI is reshaping jobs and the skills they require. A sluggish labor market, with low rates of both hiring and firing, has made it harder for workers to climb the career ladder. Meanwhile, a steady stream of layoffs, particularly in tech, has heightened concerns about job security and forced many people to reconsider what's next. For many workers facing this brave new world, that means rethinking careers, upskilling, and entrepreneurship.
What have you done to reinvent yourself in today's job market? How is job searching going? Reach out to these reporters to share at mhoff@businessinsider.com and jzinkula@businessinsider.com.
Jon McCarty spent more than a year searching for another tech job after his contract software engineering role ended in 2022. He ended up working as a part-time cashier at Home Depot and doing handyman work on the side, drawing on skills he learned as a construction worker decades earlier.
Try a
                                  Level Up action pack.
Build a calm, realistic plan to recover from job loss and move toward your next role.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
""I started coming to grips with the idea that I will probably never get another software job,"" he said.
McCarty is among the workers whose careers have taken unexpected turns in a difficult job market — and who are trying to make the best of where those changes have led.
After being laid off from her management role at Wells Fargo, Robin Peppers Daniel struggled to find a new full-time job. To earn some income, she turned to substitute teaching, something she'd enjoyed years earlier. She considered full-time teaching while trying to build a web design business with her husband.
For Kevin Hannegan, reinvention hasn't come through one new job — but a collection of gigs.
After being laid off from his director role at a publishing company in 2024, Hannegan struggled to find full-time work. He broadened his search to other white-collar roles, but ultimately pieced together income by driving for DoorDash and taking on odd jobs for friends, including fixing deck boards and replacing ceiling fans.
He also worked on the changeover crew at the Lenovo Center in Raleigh, North Carolina, where he helped convert the arena between concerts and Carolina Hurricanes games. He was technically employed by the Hurricanes, which gave him an unexpected reason to celebrate when the team won the Stanley Cup in June: he was part of the championship organization.
After more months of unsuccessful job hunting, Hannegan decided to pause his search for a full-time role — a decision that marked a new phase of his career.
""I've accepted that part-time opportunities might be the best fit for me,"" he said.
For many workers, reinvention starts with new skills.
Last year, Tanvi Pisal began to fear that AI was coming for her product designer job. When she was laid off from her startup role last fall, her fears were confirmed: An email accompanying the cuts said they were tied to the company's rapid adoption of AI.
Even before she was laid off, Pisal began spending more time learning about AI to improve her long-term career prospects. She kept at it after losing her job and even after landing a contract role at a Big Tech company, spending as much as 15 hours a week experimenting with AI tools and attending workshops.
""I don't want to be in a position a few years from now where I realize the industry moved forward and I didn't make the effort to keep learning alongside it,"" said Pisal, 29, who lives in San Jose.
For others, reinvention has meant building something of their own.
Bonnie Chiurazzi decided to try entrepreneurship after job searching post-layoff wasn't working out. She founded a market research firm, Vibe Insights Lab, drawing on her professional expertise.
She said reinventing herself from a corporate worker to a laid-off worker to a small-business owner felt like a large experiment, one she's happy to be trying, since it has improved her health. Chiurazzi said the corporate environment was becoming too stressful to handle, partly because of the persistent threat of layoffs. If she landed a new job, she would worry about being on the chopping block again.
She's learning what it means to make tough decisions and judgment calls as a business owner, but she loves having more control over outcomes and not worrying about her job security.
""I didn't realize how much work it was going to be,"" she said. ""I went into it really confident, and I came out humbled, but it was worth it.""
Chiurazzi loves that she can pick who she works with and help generate income for them in a tough job market. She's happy she doesn't have to deal with employers' push to use AI.
""It feels like the best time to continue to experiment because I don't feel like I'm missing out on anything,"" she said.

2,2026-08-09,"Your old Ikea bookshelf is part of a growing secondhand market.
Ikea has spent five years growing its buyback and resell division, which pays customers for their old Ikea-brand furniture in store credit and resells it at the chain's stores.
In September, buyback and resell will be available in every full-format US Ikea store when the retailer starts offering the option at its Brooklyn store, said Bryan Walker, country product quality manager for Ikea US.
For shoppers, buying secondhand has long been a way to save money across a range of products. It's also become a bigger business over the last several years, from side-hustlers selling clothes on marketplaces like Depop to major brands like REI selling used outdoor gear.
Used furniture has a particular attraction because of the high price of buying new pieces, and customers can often find designs not readily seen in large showrooms. Nowadays, sites like Facebook Marketplace and Craigslist are packed with used furniture from all eras.
At Ikea, the buyback program has taken back about 53,000 items over the past five years, Walker said. Customers get a quote showing how much Ikea will pay them for their furniture on Ikea's website. Then, they can bring it into a store to drop it off and get their credit.
For a large black Kallax bookcase with minor scratches, for example, Ikea will pay $69.65 at a store in the Washington, DC area, according to the website. An ""as new"" version would be worth about $10 more, while a ""well-used"" bookcase would earn about $10 less. A new version of the bookcase sells for almost $300.

Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
Ikea employees inspect the items that the company buys back. In some cases, the furniture needs cleaning or repair before the company puts it up for sale at in the As-Is section, Walker said.
Many of the items that the stores repurchase are used — and therefore cheaper — versions of what it sells new in the stores.
That appeals to customers who are trying to save money, Walker said. ""I can't have a conversation about secondhand without talking about affordability,"" he told Business Insider.
Other items attract more design-focused shoppers. Walker said that some Ikea stores have bought back pieces from its Stockholm collection, a higher-priced line that includes a sofa that costs $2,000 new.
A couple of months ago, one Ikea store bought back the program's oldest piece of furniture to date: A post-modern Signatur executive desk that Ikea started selling in the 1980s — the same decade it opened its first US stores.
Pieces like the desk appeal to customers who are looking for a specific style, Walker said. ""It's just the perfect encapsulation of really simple form,"" he said.
Customers who participate in the buyback program visit an Ikea store 50% more on average in the six months afterward, Walker said — ""a really significant impact"" that creates more loyal customers, he said.
""A lot of businesses see secondhand as a side business,"" Walker said. ""I do see this as a growth area for us.""

3,2026-08-09,"The appeal of compression boots is easy to understand: After an intense workout or a long run, these sleeves wrap around your legs and mimic the squeezing sensation of a massage. The sequential air pressure is meant to treat delayed onset muscle soreness (DOMS) and improve circulation, helping blood, oxygen, and nutrients through tired muscles.
Unlike many wellness gadgets that make big promises with little evidence behind them, intermittent pneumatic compression has been used for decades in medical and rehabilitation settings. Today, brands like Hyperice and Therabody have created at-home systems designed for everyone from marathon runners to workers who spend long hours on their feet.
But with prices ranging from a few hundred to well over $1,000—like our top pick, the Therabody JetBoots Pro Plus—and add-ons like heat and red light therapy, it can be difficult to know which boots are worth the investment. I tested the best compression boots to find the models that deliver the ideal combination of comfort, performance, and customization.
For more muscle recovery tools, check out our guides to the Best Massage Guns, Best Massagers, and Best Therabody Devices.
Love WIRED? Add us as a preferred source on Google to see more of us.
Therabody
Best Buy
Hyperice
Hyperice
Therabody
Therabody
Amazon
Best Buy
The Therabody JetBoots Pro Plus are the most feature-packed recovery boots I tested, combining pneumatic compression, vibrations, and infrared LED light therapy in one wireless system.
Why WIRED recommends: The compression and vibrating pairing is therapeutic, to say the least; it consistently left my legs feeling less stiff after workouts. Everything is controlled from an LCD screen built into the lead boot, so there’s no separate controller to keep track of. You can tailor every session to your preference, with durations ranging from 10 to 60 minutes in five-minute increments and manual controls to adjust both pressure—measured in millimeters of mercury (mmHg)—and vibration intensity. You can also choose from four compression modes and eight preset routines.
The trade-offs: Therabody doesn’t publish key specs about the LEDs, and I didn’t notice a significant difference in my post-workout recovery that I could attribute to the infrared light therapy. The boots also don’t connect to the Therabody app, which may disappoint some users, but I didn’t find it necessary. At $1,150, these are also among the priciest compression boots on the market.
Buy if: You want the most premium, customizable recovery system available and don’t mind the splurge. They’re especially suited for athletes and anyone who exercises regularly, or for people managing conditions like lymphedema, deep vein thrombosis (DVT), or chronic venous insufficiency (CVI).
Therabody
Best Buy
Target
Therabody
If all you want is effective pneumatic compression, the Therabody JetBoots Prime delivers the essentials at a more affordable price point.
Why WIRED recommends: For half the cost of the JetBoots Pro Plus, the Prime skips the vibration and infrared LED therapies but still offers four compression levels (25, 50, 75, and 100 mmHg) and three session lengths (20, 40, or 60 minutes), all controlled from a built-in panel on the boot. They’re wireless compression boots, offer up to three hours of battery life, and can even be used while charging. Weighing about 6 pounds, they’re surprisingly portable and fold down into the included drawstring backpack.
The trade-offs: Customization is limited compared to pricier models. While the boots are foldable, they’re still bulky enough that squeezing them into the drawstring bag can be a bit of a hassle.
Buy if: You want an affordable, no-frills pair of compression boots that nail the basics. They’re a great choice for first-time buyers or anyone who doesn’t want the extras like vibration or red-light therapy.
Hyperice
Hyperice
Best Buy
Amazon
If you want the recovery benefits of compression therapy without investing in full-length boots, the Normatec Go is the easiest system to live with.
Why WIRED recommends: These calf sleeves ditch the hoses and external control unit in favor of a wearable design. You can recover while making dinner, working from home, or walking around the airport. Each sleeve delivers 360-degree dynamic air compression across three overlapping zones with seven intensity levels. They’re compact, TSA-approved, and light enough to pack in a carry-on or personal item. They’re also compatible with the Hyperice app.
The trade-offs: Since the Normatec Go only targets your calves, you won’t get the full leg compression that traditional boots provide. They also don’t include a storage case, which feels like a necessity for a product designed to be portable.
Buy if: You travel frequently, want a recovery device you can use on the go, or are looking to reduce calf fatigue without packing a full-size pair of recovery boots.
Hyperice
Hyperice
REI
Best Buy
Most compression systems focus on your legs, but the Normatec Elite Hips targets your hips and glutes, which are just as prone to tightness.
Why WIRED recommends: Two overlapping air pumps deliver even, consistent pressure across your hips, glutes, IT bands, and lower back, without leaving noticeable gaps in coverage. They feature seven intensity levels, making it just as useful for loosening up after a long run as it is for activating your hips before a workout. The Elite Hips are also wireless and offer up to four hours of battery life.
The trade-offs: They’re not a replacement for compression boots. While they provide some relief to your upper quads and hamstrings, their primary focus is the hips and glutes.
Buy if: You regularly struggle with tight hips after activities like running or cycling, or you want to improve hip mobility and range of motion. They’re also worth considering if you’re looking for a noninvasive remedy to help manage conditions like sciatica or hip arthritis—just be sure to consult your health care provider first.
Hyperboot by Nike × Hyperice for $799: These high-top, sneaker-like foot massagers combine targeted air compression and heat therapy for your heels and ankles, letting you move around while recovering. That said, WIRED contributor Kristin Canning found the arch support lacking and the battery life short (roughly 60 to 90 minutes), so she wouldn’t recommend wearing them for long walks. The ranged sizing also makes it harder to find a secure fit, especially for a product that’s meant to be worn like footwear. At $799, I’d rather invest in a pair of full-length compression boots.
Urevo AI-Powered Wireless Recovery Boots for $780: These compression boots offer a high range of 80 to 180 mmHg; that’s great if you’ve found other options too gentle, but I found the highest settings uncomfortably intense and ultimately counterproductive. Urevo’s companion app offers 32 massage modes tailored to different sports and workouts, which is the most I've seen offered in my testing. These boots also generate AI-powered recovery summaries, though I found them to be pointless. The one-size-fits-most design is another drawback; it accommodates taller users well, but it can feel long if you’re short.
Urevo AI-Powered Wireless Relax Massager for $250: Like the Hyperice Normatec Go, these are calf sleeves, but they incorporate vibrations and heat therapy for a more therapeutic session. Unfortunately, I found the fit constricting and uncomfortably tight once the compression kicked in, which detracted from the overall experience. At their full retail price of $250, I’d still recommend spending a bit more for the Normatec Go. The good news is that they’re almost always on sale. At around $70 (as they were at the time of writing this), they’re an excellent bargain.
What Am I Testing Next?
I'm testing the Hyperice Normatec 3 and the Hyperice Normatec Elite.

5,2026-08-09,"Monticah Hawkins, left, and Melissa Kristin in their MySpace days
For Monticah Hawkins, a 31-year-old real-estate agent in Atlanta, MySpace wasn't just a website â it was a ritual. 
""You could be authentic on MySpace,"" she says. ""Upload raw images, no filters. You could have snot coming out your nose and nobody would judge."" 
MySpace, once the world's biggest social network, is flirting with a comeback. Its owners, brothers Tim and Chris Vanderhook, say they plan to relaunch the platform, though no date or product details have been announced. 
A spokesperson for Viant, which owns MySpace, says the company does not have any updates to share ""at this time."" 
But just the idea of a comeback has stirred nostalgia among people who remember the MySpace days of more than 20 years ago as a place where personal pages were messy, loud, and deeply individual. 
It has also caught the attention of creators like Melissa Kristin, a TikTok star with more than 900,000 followers, who grew up on MySpace and remembers it as a more personal digital space. 
""It was all very much your own little world, your own corner of the internet,"" Kristin says. 
That sense of autonomy shaped how she expresses herself online today, and she believes that a revived MySpace should avoid the algorithmic, pay-to-play dynamics that dominate modern platforms. 
""It would be best served for personal connection rather than performance,"" she says. 
""Just to recapture that high we felt as millennials when we got all the friend requests and comments. It helped us feel connected."" 
MySpace launched in 2003 and quickly became a cultural hub, helping artists like Arctic Monkeys, Adele, and Nicki Minaj find early audiences. 
But its highly customised pages, auto-playing music, and busy layouts eventually became a liability. 
Facebook's cleaner, simpler design pulled users away, and MySpace struggled through redesigns and ownership changes before fading from mainstream use. 
Marketing experts say any comeback will depend on learning from that history. 
Dr Alexa Fox, a marketing professor at the University of Akron, says the platform was a ""first mover"" in social media, but one that became too complex for many users. 
She believes MySpace's original strengths â music, creativity, and personal expression â could still resonate, but only with more accessible design. 
""It could be more user-friendly, with less coding involved,"" she says.
Alex Mills, a social media strategist who has led campaigns for Spotify and Adobe, says the very thing people are nostalgic for, a feed without algorithms, was also ""the thing that killed it."" 
""People still want a feed,"" Mills says. ""They just want a better one than the ones optimising for rage bait."" 
Kevin McClary, head of performance marketing at Gorilla 76, believes MySpace should not try to compete directly with Meta, TikTok, or X. 
""They can be successful and sustainable even as a niche platform,"" he says, arguing it should be rooted in 2000s culture. 
McClary says leaning into MySpace's music identity could be a smart move, from profile songs to 2000s-themed festivals and merchandise. 
He believes the audience could be broader than former users, with younger generations embracing the early-2000s aesthetics they never experienced firsthand. 
""You see younger generations now buying old point-and-shoot digital cameras, MP3 players, even film cameras,"" McClary says. ""They clearly have an interest in the past."" 
MySpace could meet users where that trend is growing by becoming a place rooted in a ""specific time,"" he says. He noted that creators making 2000s-style content on other platforms are often at the ""mercy"" of algorithms. 
Fox agrees that differentiation is essential. 
""MySpace shouldn't hang their hat on features other platforms already dominate,"" Fox says, pointing to Reels and short-form videos. 
Popular formats can be added, she says, but they should not define the platform. 
She also sees an opportunity to offer a calmer experience. Today's feeds, she notes, are ""algorithmic-driven AI, constantly in your face."" 
A platform where users have more control, or simply feel less overwhelmed, could stand out in a crowded market. 
For fans like Hawkins and Kristin, the appeal is emotional as much as practical. Both say they would return if MySpace kept its original spirit. 
For now, its comeback exists mostly as a promise â a reminder of a time when social media felt a little less polished and a lot more personal. 
""Are you still gonna have that same feeling?"" Hawkins says. ""Are you still gonna feel like, 'oh my god, somebody loves me?'""

Home > Kitchen & Household
I asked chefs about the snacks, ingredients and guilty pleasure foods they can't stop buying.
August 9, 2026, 7:00 am ET
While the world’s top chefs have direct access to premium ingredients, complex recipes and state-of-the-art appliances, they, too, fall for guilty-pleasure foods, shortcut ingredients and other foods nobody tells us novices about. 
Until now…
To get the inside scoop on the best online food buys, I asked eight chefs to share the snacks, condiments and cooking must-haves that bring them the most joy when not on the line and firing up other people’s orders. Here are their picks. 
There’s no denying the allure of a salty, crunchy potato chip, and umami-packed Torres black truffle variety is a staple for Executive Chef Michael Quigley of the Fairmont Sonoma Mission Inn and Spa.
“I first noticed these at the grocery store several years ago and was instantly hooked,” Quigley said. “When my local store stopped carrying them, I naturally had to turn to Amazon because I genuinely can’t live without them.”
“They are ridiculously good: super salty, which I love, and completely addictive,” he added, noting that while best enjoyed on their own, he often pairs them with homemade tuna salad. “Do I know they are probably loaded with artificial truffle flavoring? Absolutely. As a chef, should I know better? Probably. Do I care? Not even a little.” 
Anchovies may be polarizing for the everyday person, but not for Mark Ladner, executive chef at Babbo in New York. 
“We always have these Sicilian anchovies from Agostino Recca on hand to anchor our menu, celebrating a coastal, southern character that gives our dishes a layered depth,” he shared.  
While some mealtime essentials such as proteins and produce must remain fresh, having this high-quality brined fish on demand helps Ladner to “stay calibrated, rather than trying to source them directly every few weeks.”
“There is a misconception that to make a dish compelling, you have to overmanipulate it or use 20 different steps,” he said. “In reality, it’s about prioritizing high-quality ingredients that combine the fresh produce you grab at the market and the heavy-hitting staples you can import.”
For the chef on the go, a high-protein smoothie can quickly satiate any intense hunger craving. 
“As a chef and restaurateur, my schedule is anything but predictable,” said Ryan Salter, co-founder and chief product officer of Breadless, a Detroit-based, fast-casual spot reimagining carb-heavy sandwiches. “Between early prep, catering events, menu development, and running restaurants, there are plenty of days when I’m feeding everyone else before I have a chance to feed myself,” he said.
“Having these protein smoothie pouches on Subscribe & Save means I always have a nutritious option within reach, whether I’m heading into the kitchen or driving between events,” he said, raving over the pouches, which can be tossed in a bag or stored in an office. “They’re gluten-free, made with real fruit, contain 12 grams of protein, and don’t require refrigeration, so they fit my lifestyle perfectly.” 
“As a chef, I’m always looking for ways to build flavor, and this is one ingredient that consistently delivers,” said Phillip Dewayne, chef and owner of Park and Cherry in Memphis. “I use it to enrich soups, pan sauces, rice, braised greens, and even mashed potatoes. It adds the rich, slow-simmered depth you’d expect from homemade stock when time is limited.” 
Rather than relying on cubes or high-sodium cans of broth, only one teaspoon of the jarred gold can immediately season eight ounces of water. 
“It’s a pantry staple I never want to be without because it makes everyday cooking taste more thoughtful and full of flavor,” Dewayne added. 
For Raúl Duarte, chef of a soon-to-announced Shanghainese restaurant in New York, That’s It fruit bars have become “lifesavers” that he can take anywhere. 
“They have saved me from many moments where I feel the need for a snack,” he said. “While the apple and strawberry fruit bar consists of a two-ingredient bar made straight up from the fruit itself, you are left with satisfaction without any mess.”
“As chefs, sometimes we have the bad habit of eating whatever is in our reach while we’re at work. The That’s It fruit bars are small enough to fit in your pocket,” he said, gushing over the snack. 
Aside from the apple and strawberry combo, Duarte is a big fan of the spicy iteration featuring apple, mango and chili that can “lead to fruit addiction.” 
Honey’s versatility has made it a pantry mainstay for generations, but it’s Rare Hawaiian’s Organic Kiawe Honey that’s infused with lilikoi that has Chef Ahu Hettema of Istanbul Hawaii singing its praises. 
“It’s the one ingredient I reach for almost every day,” Hettema said. “The first thing that gets me is the texture. It is silky, creamy, and luxurious. Then the flavor unfolds. The rich, buttery depth of kiawe honey is lifted by the bright, tangy burst of lilikoi, creating a balance that’s both vibrant and deeply satisfying. Once you’ve tasted it, ordinary honey feels one-dimensional.”
Those seeking a bit of culinary inspiration can scoop it over Greek yogurt with toasted macadamias, sliced bananas and shaved dark chocolate for a morning indulgence. 
“It [also] finds its way into our baklava syrup, vinaigrettes, pan sauces, dessert syrups, cocktails, and even my Turkish tea instead of sugar,” she explained. “Every time I use it, I discover another pairing that just works.”
A good bottle of olive oil or two is a kitchen must-have for cooking, sauteing, and finishing. While a midpriced oil will suffice for marinades and roasting large amounts of food, a splurge bottle is worth it for dipping, finishing and other delicate applications.
“Exceptional cooking starts with exceptional ingredients. Technique is important, but even the most refined technique can’t replace true quality,” said Thomas Griese, corporate executive chef of JING in Scottsdale. 
“Laudemio is an olive oil I reach for. Whether I’m finishing grilled vegetables, dressing a salad or adding the final touch to fresh fish, pasta or meat, a great olive oil brings a dish to life. It’s not just a finishing ingredient — it’s also an expression of balance, flavor and restraint,” he said. 
Ismet Sahin, executive chef of Agora Restaurant Group in the Washington, D.C. area, echoes these sentiments. “I never skip Laconiko extra virgin olive oil,” Sahin divulged. As a Mediterranean chef from Turkey, olive oil isn’t just another ingredient — “it’s the foundation of almost everything I cook.”
“I’ve tasted countless oils over the years, but Laconiko stands out because it’s incredibly fresh, balanced and flavorful without being overpowering. It’s also the extra virgin olive oil we proudly use in our kitchens, so it’s something I lean on both professionally and personally,” he revealed.

The mechanical keyboard market has quietly split into two distinct camps. On one side, you’ve got premium enthusiast boards built around sound profiles, gasket mounts, and typing feel. On the other, gaming keyboards chasing faster actuation and competitive response. The trouble is that most people don’t belong exclusively to one camp, and keyboards that try to serve both rarely pull off either particularly well.
GlowHint designed the KT75 around exactly that frustration. It’s a 75% Hall Effect magnetic keyboard with 84 keys, built on the premise that you shouldn’t have to choose between a board that feels great on your desk and one that keeps up in a competitive game. That premise goes beyond marketing language; it’s woven into the keyboard’s physical structure.
Designer: GLowHint
Click Here to Buy Now: $299 $610 (55% off). Hurry, only 88/100 left!

Traditional mechanical switches use metal contacts that only register two states: pressed or not. Hall Effect switches work differently, using magnetic sensors to continuously track a key stem’s exact position throughout its entire travel. The keyboard always knows how far each key has moved, and that continuous awareness is what enables adjustable actuation, a 0.01mm rapid trigger, and a more responsive experience overall.

Where the KT75 gets physically interesting is the Dual Mount Structure. With the gaskets installed, keystrokes feel more supple and forgiving, with a deeper sound character that suits long typing sessions well. Remove those gaskets and the whole feel sharpens, trading comfort for the firmer, more direct response that competitive gaming demands. It’s a physical configuration, not a menu setting.

That design care carries through the rest of the construction. The KT75 uses a CNC aluminum alloy chassis with an FR4 plate and a six-layer acoustic dampening structure that includes PORON foam, a PET sound pad, and bottom dampening. The switches actuate at just 30gf and bottom out at 50gf, keeping effort light without the mushy feel that lighter switches sometimes produce.

On the gaming side, the KT75 scans every key 16,000 times per second and communicates with the computer at 8,000Hz over a wired connection. Rapid Trigger removes the fixed reset point, so movements as small as 0.01mm register as fresh inputs, and Snap Tap handles conflicting directional inputs by prioritizing the most recent one. Both are especially noticeable in fast-paced shooters where every fraction of a second counts.

For everyday use, the compact 84-key layout trims hand travel without dropping essential keys, and the macro system lets you assign complex commands to a single keystroke. The more interesting layer is depth-based behavior, where a lighter press and a full press can each trigger different actions. It’s a quietly useful feature that makes the keyboard feel more thoughtful than its key count suggests.

GlowHint built a 1.47-inch OLED smart display into the board, capable of showing status information or custom content including GIFs, pixel art, and logos uploaded through the companion software. All of that, along with per-key RGB across 16.8 million colors, key remapping, switch tuning, and macro configuration, is handled through GlowHint Hub. The switches are also hot-swappable, so the board can grow with your preferences.

The KT75 supports three connection modes: 2.4 GHz wireless, Bluetooth, and USB-C wired. The wired connection unlocks the full 8,000Hz polling rate, while 2.4 GHz handles gaming without Bluetooth latency concerns. The dual 4,000mAh batteries are rated up to 95 hours wirelessly, though that drops to around 13 hours with default RGB on, which is the more realistic figure for most sessions.


The through line across all these choices is consistency. GlowHint didn’t try to make the KT75 do everything at once; each decision, from the swappable gasket layers to the magnetic switch technology to the configurable display, adds a specific kind of flexibility that serves a different part of the same day. It ends up as a keyboard that earns its complexity without making you feel it.

By the mid-2010s, gaming had become an important battleground in the lucrative field of consumer technology. Companies across the industry were competing to win over players with products designed around a growing and increasingly competitive gaming culture. Logitech, founded in Switzerland in 1981, had already established itself as a major player in PC gaming peripherals with its Logitech G brand, but it was keen to expand further by gaining a stronger foothold in the console market. It was this ambition that led the company to take an interest in California-based Astro Gaming, a leading brand in gaming headsets. In 2017, Logitech made a move, acquiring Astro in a deal worth $85 million.
Founded in 2006, Astro had spent more than a decade building a name for itself in the world of gaming when Logitech came knocking. The smaller company was already well known for its premium headsets, particularly the A40 and A50, which had become popular among everyone from gaming enthusiasts to esports competitors. For Logitech's leadership, the appeal was not simply in acquiring another hardware line, but in gaining a brand with an established identity and a loyal audience. When Logitech announced its acquisition, it described Astro as ""a perfect complement to Logitech G's focus on PC gaming."" At that point, with Astro now under its wing, the question was whether Logitech would be able to strengthen its position in console gaming without diluting the brand that had made Astro so valuable in the first place.
Many years after Logitech's acquisition of Astro Gaming, the brand continues to exist, but its products now appear as part of Logitech's own website. With Logitech keen to make the most of Astro's popular profile, the flagship A50 headset line also continues to this day, alongside newer products like the A50 X Lightspeed.
Astro headsets continue to go up against high-end alternatives from the likes of Razer, Turtle Beach, and SteelSeries, and still receive praise for their build quality and comfort. Even so, the brand's premium positioning means that its flagship products still have to justify their price in the face of strong offerings from rivals. The flagship A50 X Lightspeed, for example, costs a hefty $360, placing it alongside top-tier gaming headphones such as SteelSeries' Arctis Nova Pro Wireless and Turtle Beach's Stealth Pro. At that price, some gamers looking for a new headset may wonder whether Astro's brand legacy alone is enough to justify choosing the A50 X Lightspeed over those competing high-end headsets.
Measuring the success of Logitech's acquisition depends on how it's judged. From Logitech's perspective, the deal appears to have achieved its strategic goals, giving the company a stronger foothold in console gaming while preserving Astro as a respected premium headset brand. However, while Astro has remained part of Logitech's large portfolio of tech brands, the company's identity has evolved since the acquisition. In the mid-2010s, Astro was positioned as a specialist console gaming headset brand with strong links to esports, while today it operates within Logitech's broader gaming ecosystem. Ultimately, the acquisition looks like a success for Logitech, but it does illustrate the challenge of integrating an established gaming brand without losing some of the qualities that made it special.

11,2026-08-09,"The AI boom caused a huge spike in PC hardware demand, which, in turn, drove up prices. Everything from GPUs to sticks of RAM saw price increases, to the point where it became unfeasible for gaming hardware manufacturers to continue selling their devices at their current prices. So, pretty much across the board, all gaming hardware manufacturers increased their prices.
However, it seems the price hikes have had an inverse effect on what the companies wanted. Fewer sales were expected, but less profit was the real stinger —I'm not 100% surprised.
 The issue came to my attention when I reported on how badly the Steam Deck price hike had supposedly affected sales of the device. The entry-level Steam Deck OLED had a price increase from $549 to $789, and the website Boiling Steam investigated how badly that affected its sales.
Valve hasn't revealed official sales figures, so Boiling Steam did some very smart napkin math based on the Steam Deck's position on the Top Sellers chart before and after the price increase. What it found was pretty dire:
Metric
Pre-Adjustment (2025)
Post-Adjustment (Mid-2026)

$6.0M to $10.0M

~$835
+54%

11k to 18k units
1.4k to 3k units
≈ -82%
Yes, that's an 82% decrease in units sold per week, and a 72% decrease in money coming in. The Steam Deck went from selling a maximum predicted count of 18,000 units a week to just 3,000. These numbers are by no means a guarantee that Valve is making less money now than it did before the price increase, but if this data is even remotely accurate, things look really bad.
Every day now, a new PS5 or Xbox makes undeniably more sense.
As for the world of consoles, things didn't look so great for them, either. The PlayStation 5 saw price increases, and while we don't have any hard data to tell us how much it affected sales, what we have heard sounds really bad.
IGN ran a piece on just how damaging these price increases were. The month after Sony increased the PS5 price, the company saw a 58% decrease in hardware sales, the worst sales for May since 2000. For perspective, 2000 was the year after the launch of the PlayStation 2 in Japan and a few months before it launched in the EU and US.
Not only that, but PS5 spending fell 43% year-on-year. So, not only were people not buying PS5s as much as they used to, but the people already with PS5s were beginning to tighten their wallets, too.
It's not perfect, but it's a solution.
 So, why are sales so abysmal post-price hike? In my opinion, it's because gaming has gotten very, very expensive lately. Yes, the hardware crisis has caused the devices themselves to increase in price, but we're also seeing the games themselves asking for more money.
As a result, we're seeing a nasty pincer attack where both sides of the gaming coin are demanding more from gamers' wallets. People in the hobby just don't have the cash to splash on a more expensive console that features more expensive games on it anymore. And the worst part is, the hardware crisis is still going, meaning that we'll likely see a few more price hikes in the future.
Gamers might not be rushing to upgrade their gaming PCs as much as they used to. And the reasons aren't surprising. 
So, you're looking at the current state of gaming. You see that consoles and high-end gaming PCs are spiraling out of control in terms of cost. Even if you scrape together the funds for one, you'll need to pay a premium for games that take advantage of that hardware. If there was nothing else there to act as a pressure release, people may very well feel forced to sink money into this gaming market.
But then you realize: you don't need to sink any money into anything, actually. After all, you have a good enough PC to run indie games. And those indie games cost a fraction of what the big leagues are asking for. For the $80 that Rockstar wants for Grand Theft Auto 6, you can buy several indie titles that will give you hours of fun. Hollow Knight: Silksong, Slay the Spire 2, Mina the Hollower, and Meccha Chameleon, to name just a few of 2026's biggest hitters.
I think this hit home for me when I picked up RV There Yet? for $8. My friends and I got 10 hours out of that game, and that was only on the first map. The game contains a whole different second map, and a third one is in the works. And trust me when I say I had a ton more fun with that game than I have with some triple-A titles.
And I haven't even touched upon free-to-play games yet. You don't need a high-end PC or deep pockets to play League of Legends, Dead by Daylight, or even Roblox. In a world where gaming hardware and software are more expensive than they've ever been, a free game that runs off a toaster is the perfect escape.
They're not exactly wrong.
With so many cheap and free games to play, people don't have to purchase triple-A games and the latest hardware to enjoy the hobby. It's why I think the price increases for hardware have backfired on the people setting it; people refuse to swallow the bitter pill of their hobby getting more expensive. Not when there are perfectly good, cheap, and free options available.

Stock charts are crucial tools for investors. Stock charts are important today because retail investors have access to the same real-time price data and charting tools that institutional investors have used. Candlestick charts, detailing open, high, low and close prices, are preferred for their visual clarity, alongside trading volume which indicates conviction behind price movements. Investors learn to identify key patterns like support and resistance levels, trendlines and reversal formations such as Head and Shoulders or Double Tops/Bottoms. Technical indicators like Simple Moving Averages and RSI further aid analysis. A systematic approach involves assessing long-term trends, marking key levels and confirming moves with volume, while always considering the broader market.
Open a stock chart for the first time, and it can feel overwhelming. But every mark is telling a simple story about buyers and sellers fighting over a price, and learning to read it is a genuinely useful skill for a new investor, whether the goal is trading individual names or just understanding why a portfolio moved the way it did last week.
I’ll explain chart types, core components and the patterns and indicators chartists lean on most. None of it requires expensive software — most brokerages and free charting sites already include it. One caveat: chart reading is a tool, not a crystal ball, and works best alongside a broader plan, whether that’s a core position in beginner index funds or research into individual names.
Stock charts matter more today than they did a decade ago because retail investors now have the same real-time price data and charting tools that institutional investors use. 
The 2021 GameStop rally is a prime example: the stock surged from under $20 to more than $400 in a matter of weeks, fueled largely by social media and retail buying rather than changes in the company's fundamentals. In situations like these, a chart can quickly reveal whether a move is building sustained momentum or showing signs of exhaustion. Reading charts helps investors separate genuine trends from short-lived speculation and provides an objective check on whether a holding still fits their investment strategy.
A stock chart is simply a picture of price over time. Every chart plots the same underlying data — what a stock traded for, and when — but different chart types show more or less detail. 
The three you’ll see most are line, bar and candlestick charts. A line chart is the simplest, connecting closing prices into a single trend line. Bar and candlestick charts both display four data points per period (open, high, low, close). Still, candlesticks use color and shape to make that data easier to read at a glance, which is why they’re the industry standard for technical analysis.
Regardless of chart type, most stock charts share the same building blocks: the axes and timeframe that frame the data, the anatomy of an individual candlestick, and the trading volume beneath the price panel. Together they show scale, sentiment and conviction.
Every chart has two axes: price runs vertically, time runs horizontally. The timeframe you select changes the story, though. A one-day chart shows intraday swings that mean little for a long-term holding, while a five-year chart smooths out daily noise and shows whether a company has actually built value over time. A long-term investor can get a more useful signal from a weekly or monthly view than a five-minute chart built for short-term traders, especially for something like beginner dividend stocks meant to be held for years.
A single candlestick packs four numbers into one shape: the open, the close and the period's high and low. The thick body spans open to close; thin lines above and below — the wicks — mark the highest and lowest prices touched. Color does the rest: platforms shade a candle green when it is closed higher than it opened, red when lower. A long body with short wicks means one side controlled the period; a small body with long wicks on both ends, a doji, suggests indecision. That detail is why candlestick charts — devised by 18th-century Japanese rice trader Munehisa Homma and later introduced to the West by analyst Steve Nison — remain the industry's preferred format.
Most charts display a bar graph beneath the price panel showing trading volume, total shares that changed hands each period. Volume measures conviction: a stock that jumps 5% on unusually heavy volume suggests broad participation and staying power, while the same jump on thin volume might just reflect a handful of trades. Volume also confirms or casts doubt on a pattern — a breakout on a volume spike is more reliable than the same breakout on light volume.
Once you're comfortable reading candles and volume, the next step is recognizing patterns that form across many periods — a structured way to gauge where a stock might head next.
Support is a price level where a stock has historically found enough buyers to stop a decline; resistance is the opposite, a level where selling has repeatedly capped a rally. The CMT Association, which certifies Chartered Market Technicians, describes these zones as collective market memory: prices pause where trading previously stalled. 
For example, if a stock repeatedly fails to break above $100, that price often becomes a resistance level as investors who previously bought near $100 use rallies to sell and break even. If the stock eventually closes decisively above $100 and continues higher, that former resistance frequently becomes a new support level, with buyers stepping in when the price pulls back toward $100. The reverse can also occur after a breakdown. These price levels give investors concrete points to monitor instead of relying on a vague sense that a stock feels ""cheap"" or ""expensive.""
A trendline connects a series of higher lows in an uptrend or lower highs in a downtrend; a stock bouncing between a consistent ceiling and floor is range-bound instead. 
For example, Nvidia's multiyear advance featured a series of higher highs and higher lows, allowing technicians to draw an upward trendline that remained intact despite periodic pullbacks. Investors following the trend generally viewed those pullbacks as opportunities rather than signs to sell. 
By contrast, a stock that repeatedly trades between two price levels without establishing new highs or lows is considered range-bound. Trendlines work best as a first filter—many technicians avoid betting against an established trend, on the idea that a trend in motion stays in motion until something meaningful changes.
A head and shoulders pattern forms when a stock rallies to a peak (left shoulder), pulls back, rallies to a higher peak (the head), then rallies a third time to a lower peak (right shoulder)—a sign an uptrend may be losing momentum. 
For example, if a stock peaks at $90, then $100, then only $92 before falling below support at $80 (the ""neckline""), many technicians view it as a bearish reversal. Double tops (an M) and double bottoms (a W) are simpler reversal patterns. A stock that fails twice near $50 may be forming a double top, while one that rebounds twice from $30 may be forming a double bottom. All three patterns carry more weight when confirmed by above-average trading volume.
Charts become more powerful paired with indicators. The simple moving average (SMA) is usually the first one beginners learn: it averages closing prices over a set number of periods, such as 50 or 200 days, smoothing out noise so the trend is easier to see — a price crossing above its 200-day SMA is often read as bullish, a cross below as bearish. The relative strength index (RSI), from J. Welles Wilder's 1978 book New Concepts in Technical Trading Systems, is another staple: a momentum oscillator scored 0 to 100 that flags when a stock might be overbought (above 70) or oversold (below 30). Neither works well alone — pair a trend indicator with a momentum indicator before making a call.
Reading individual components is one thing; a repeatable process is what makes chart reading useful:
Each step narrows your focus: the long-term trend keeps you from mistaking a bounce for a reversal, while support, resistance and volume tell you whether an entry or exit makes sense. Repeat the process on positions you already own, not just before a new trade.
The most common beginner mistake is piling on indicators — five or six overlays on one chart usually produce conflicting signals, not more insight. Master one trend and one momentum indicator before adding more. The second pitfall is treating any pattern as a guarantee: RSI can stay ""overbought"" for weeks in a strong trend, and breakouts fail. Confirmation bias — noticing signals that confirm what you already believe while dismissing the rest — costs more than any single bad trade. Chart reading works best alongside a company’s fundamentals, not in place of them.
What Is The Best Stock Chart Type For Absolute Beginners?
The candlestick chart is the best starting point. It shows the same open, high, low and close data as a bar chart, but color-coded bodies make bullish and bearish periods easier to spot than a line chart's single trend line.
What Is The Difference Between Technical Analysis And Fundamental Analysis?
Technical analysis studies price and volume patterns to gauge sentiment and timing, while fundamental analysis evaluates a company's financial health — revenue, earnings, debt — to estimate what a stock is worth.
How Do You Identify Support And Resistance On A Stock Chart?
Look for price levels where a stock has reversed direction multiple times: a floor it has bounced off more than once (support) or a ceiling it has repeatedly failed to break (resistance).
Can You Rely Only On Stock Charts To Make Investment Decisions?
Charts show what a stock's price has done, not necessarily why, or what the company is worth. Most professionals pair chart reading with fundamental research and broader market context before deciding."
14,2026-08-09,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Thomas Flohr, the 65-year-old founder, chairman, and CEO of VistaJet, based in St. Moritz, Switzerland, about his daily routine. It's been edited for length and clarity.
I started my career at a Chicago-based technology firm in 1985 and rose through the ranks. I later acquired most of the company's European operations through my company, Comprendium Investment.
My frequent business travel in the early 2000s exposed me to the shortcomings of private aviation. At the time, I found the private jet experience to be inconsistent and underwhelming. The service quality didn't match the level of luxury that clients paying $10,000 to $15,000 an hour expected. I didn't see a good solution in the market and initially thought about paying for fractional jet ownership before deciding against it.
Business Insider's Power Hours series gives readers an inside look at how powerful leaders in business structure their workday. Reach out to editor Lauryn Haas to share your daily routine.
After exploring all other options, I bought a Learjet 60 in 2003, and that plane became the foundation for starting VistaJet in 2004. Vista is a private aviation subscription service that provides access to over 270 customized business jets for clients in 187 countries.
My primary job is building client relationships and setting the company's strategic direction. Here's what a typical day in my life looks like.
Our company is headquartered in Dubai, but I travel frequently and usually work from any of our 25 offices worldwide, including my home office in St. Moritz. I wake up at different times, depending on where I am. If I'm in America, I start earlier to sync with Europe. In the Middle East, I work late into the night to overlap with American and European business hours.
In St. Moritz, my mornings start around 8 a.m. I shower, grab coffee, and ease into the day. I don't eat breakfast.
Try a
                                Level Up action pack.
Build a calm, realistic plan to recover from job loss and move toward your next role.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
I prefer not to reach for my phone right away. About 45 minutes after my coffee, I'll respond to overnight emails and messages before diving into the day's activities.
I maintain several offices in different locations so my executives can use them when I'm not around. If I'm home, I'll work from my home office. If I'm on the road, I'll work on the plane or at my destination.
Sometimes, I work from the hotel in the morning and knock out key business tasks. If I'm in Dubai, London, New York, or a city with a VistaJet office, I'll head there in the afternoon for team and client meetings.
I often sit where the action is with the team or join executive committee members in their offices.
I prioritize client meetings before internal ones and don't believe internal meetings should take more than 20 to 30 minutes unless there's a compelling reason.
When clients call me directly, it's usually because they're about to make a significant decision, like buying a plane or finalizing a global flight plan. I also work on product development, brainstorming new services and product feature updates between calls and meetings.
As a founder, I'm still very hands-on, and I'm in constant communication with my finance and operations teams.
For lunch, I keep it very light and healthy. I usually eat vegetable soup, a fruit platter, or a light Japanese sashimi dish.
If I'm having a client lunch meeting, I prefer to eat at restaurants that use high-quality ingredients. Some of my favorite restaurants include Scott's in Mayfair, London, and Gaia in Dubai. When I eat out, I mostly get grilled seafood and boiled potatoes — nothing fancy.
The afternoon often means syncing with our US and European teams. In our business, there's no shortage of unusual requests that require creative solutions. One memorable request involved helping a client run three marathons on three continents in one week. It was a significant logistical undertaking, but we successfully pulled it off for the client.
Another request involved helping a corporate client coordinate the flight of executives from four continents to a board meeting in India, ensuring they all arrived precisely on time and returned home safely afterward.
If there's a gap between meetings, I'll take a 10-15-minute break to walk outside and get some fresh air. I try to prioritize these short breaks to clear my head and reset, but most of the time, the day runs nonstop.
By 6 or 6:30 p.m., I head to the gym and spend an hour training. Once I'm done working out, I hit the steam room for 15 to 20 minutes to relax and recover. Staying fit keeps me sharp.
I'm a firm believer in an integrated life. I don't view work and life as separate entities that need to be balanced against each other. Instead, I see everything in life as one endurance effort, whether in business or sports like car racing.
The time I eat dinner varies depending on my location. In some countries, I eat at 7 p.m.; in others, at 9 or 9:30 p.m. I adapt to the time zone and cultural norms. When I'm home, I eat dinner around 8:30 p.m., but I'm always available for client calls if needed.
I enjoy cooking and dining with friends and family. I have a bowling alley in my home, so we head downstairs for a few rounds after dinner. It's a great way to unwind and stir up friendly competition.
With so much digital stimulation during the day, I avoid screens at night. I don't watch TV, but I listen to classical music or rock from the late '70s and early '80s before bed.
I usually fall asleep around 11:30 p.m., sleeping seven hours on weeknights and nine on weekends.
Despite flying around 500 hours annually, I make time for things I enjoy. I'm a big fan of simple living and doing things in nature, like walking, hiking, and shopping at my local market for fruits and vegetables.

15,2026-08-09,"If you've ever admired someone's engagement ring, you've probably oohed and aahed over the size or shine of ""the rock,"" as many like to call it.
The diamond or other gem in an engagement ring is a key element, but it's important to remember the entire ring needs to be durable since it's typically worn every day.
Unfortunately, some of the most popular engagement ring styles throw practicality out the window in favor of aesthetics.
Business Insider spoke with private jeweler Anna P. Jay, who is based in Nantucket, Massachusetts, and Charleston, South Carolina, about engagement ring styles that may not stand the test of time.
Celebrities like Taylor Swift and Zendaya have been favoring chunkier engagement rings in recent years, but rings with very thin bands are still popular in the market. Jay said the latter can be a mistake.
""I don't like super-thin bands personally because sometimes they're not sturdy,"" Jay said.
Though thin bands create a sleek look and can make a stone appear larger, they aren't as supportive, particularly if your ring has a large carat stone.

                                    Every time Samantha Grindell Pettyjohn publishes a story, you'll get an alert straight to your inbox!
                                

                                    Stay connected to Samantha Grindell Pettyjohn and get more of their work as it publishes.
                                


                                    By clicking ""Sign up"", you agree to receive emails from Business Insider. In addition, you accept Insider's
                                    Terms of Service and
                                    Privacy Policy.
                                  
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
""When you have a larger stone on the top, it becomes top-heavy, so it'll constantly be spinning,"" Jay said. Thin rings can also bend with use and might need more prongs to keep a stone in place since there is less base support.
Jay said it's better to get a slightly thicker band so the ring will stand the test of time. Plus, an increased width isn't usually noticeable to the naked eye.
""A matter of tens of millimeters makes a huge difference,"" Jay said.
Jay said the U-prong eternity band (colloquially called an infinity ring), which has become popular in recent years, isn't that practical either.
The entire band of an eternity ring is covered in diamonds or another jewel. There are multiple styles of eternity rings, but the U-prong setting is especially popular because it has very little visible metal and focuses the attention on the gems. It's also the least practical, according to Jay.
""U-prong settings are incredibly impractical,"" Jay said. ""People like them because of the lack of metal, but I'll make it, and I've had to reset or tighten prongs after a week of wear.""
""I think jewelry should be worn, and if you can't wear it because it's too fragile, what's the point?"" she said.
In addition, eternity rings cannot be resized because the entire band is set with diamonds or another stone.
That's a problem, as most people's fingers change size throughout their lives, Jay said. Because it can't be easily resized, an eternity band may have to be reset entirely at some point.
Jay recommends halfway or even three-quarter bands as an alternative, since a partially metal band makes it easy to resize.
With all aspects of engagement rings, Jay advises people to have open conversations with their partner about what they expect from a ring.
""Make sure you do talk to your partner about what you want because it's not a cup of coffee,"" she said. ""We can't just throw it away and start again.""
""This is often the most expensive piece of jewelry a lot of people will purchase, and it's the first piece of jewelry a lot of people will purchase,"" Jay added. ""Let's make sure we nail it.""

The drivers of the pumps also need to be considered: are the pumps powered by the electric grid, by generator, by prime mover engine (what fuel?), etc.?
Either way the pumping stations are fixed, the power systems for the pumps can be easily identified, and the pumping system can be taken out by damage to either the pumps or the drivers, preferably both if you are the attacker.
My own (US) experience designing pumping stations is that an enclosed pumphouse costs at least as much as the pumping equipment. I’ve had municipal clients that insist that we design their pumps and controls to be left out in the Minnesota weather for several years because the pumphouse is too expensive- they’ll build it later when they have the funds.

However, several experimental studies have suggested that RF-EMF exposure may influence important cellular processes, including oxidative stress, redox signaling, and inflammatory responses. Increased production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) and alterations in antioxidant defense mechanisms have been observed in both in vitro and in vivo studies, indicating potential oxidative damage at the cellular level. It’s trivial to sweep out one leg of the author’s argument: there’s nothing to link Tian Junhua to Shi Zhengli. They came from very different educational backgrounds, have different research interests, and worked at unrelated institutions. That they both worked in Wuhan – a city comparable in population to NYC – is of course of little relevance.
Tian Junhua, as they acknowledge, was under the Wuhan CDC. To elaborate a little on his background, as the article didn’t do, he received an undergraduate education in entomology at the Huazhong (Middle China) Agricultural University, completing it and joining the Wuhan CDC in 2004. The focus of his work was and is on invertebrate transmission vectors – and even when he acquired an interest in bats he focused on bat parasites; this much is obvious for anyone who paid attention to the samples he collected in video the article references. For those who understand Chinese, it’s pretty clear and openly acknowledged that he was undertrained in the beginning for the fieldwork he performed, and mostly learned by doing. He was reported by contemporary Chinese media articles to be working on a PhD on the side as of mid-late 2010s. Some of the articles also note that he worked in collaboration with the Chinese (i.e. national) CDC, which comports with the listing of staff from the Chinese CDC as coauthors in his recent publications, e.g. Tick-borne agents in the fowl tick Argas persicus from northwest and northeast China
As I mentioned in a comment last week, Dr. Shi is a trained virologist who received her PhD from Montpellier-2 in France. Almost all of her working career was spent at the Wuhan Institute of Virology, a research institution directly administered by the Chinese Academy of Sciences. So to trace their places in an organization chart:
Tian: State Council (in common) -> Province of Hubei -> City of Wuhan -> Wuhan CDC
As local CDCs in China are subject to dual oversight*, there is an alternative chain for Tian:
State Council (in common) -> National Health Commission -> Chinese CDC -> Hubei CDC -> Wuhan CDC
Shi: State Council (in common) -> Chinese Academy of Sciences -> Wuhan Institute of Virology
Note that as the above chart implies, the head of the Chinese Academy of Sciences is a cabinet-level position, equal in rank to the governor of Hubei and the head of the National Health Commission.
In summary, the article is suggesting that a renowned expert doesn’t know what she’s doing in her own field of training and expertise because an under-trained low-level civil servant who happened to work in an unrelated organization in the same city didn’t. This is the sort of thing that apparently flies when you can’t tell one Chinese from another.
* to my understanding, in the dual oversight system an organization is subject to the oversight of the upper level equivalent as well as the regional government it is a part of. I say this becausen let’s say 99% of.this “creative job” wasn’t done by AI. Of these, only 16 happened to be able to multiply and form viral particles in in vitro experiments with cultured bacteria. So we have something about a 6% success ratio in in vitro culture conditions. This for a virus which is quite small in terms of genome length. About 5,600 nucleotides rank these viruses amongst the smallest ones. I think that you give me a couple of days and come with results in more or less the same range of success and I wouldn’t call it creative but playing with genomic variations over a template. Tom’s hardware comes with this outstanding conclusion:
However, because trillions of possible genomes exist, it has been statistically impossible for humans to study enough of them to know the formula for how nature puts these building blocks together to form an organism. The blocks were known, but the formula for arranging them in a way that created viable genes wasn’t. Until now.
The hype it burns. What made it possible to “put these building blocks together” in this case about 260 candidates, wasn’t AI, but pre-existing synthetic biology procedures which can now create relatively long sequences with ease and not too many mistakes. AI only created the sequence (not from scratch but from given natural templates) Synthetic biology limits such research which cannot be overcome by AI. 17,2026-08-09,"On August 10, 1874, a boy was born to a blacksmith and a seamstress in West Branch, Iowa, a boy who would one day become the 31st president of the United States. Yet, the path he took to the Oval Office was anything but typical for a head of state.
But weâre getting ahead of ourselves.
Letâs trace Herbert Hooverâs path to the presidency, one milestone at a time, and see how the Chief went from Midwest orphan to guiding the nation through the onset of the Great Depression (or at least attempting to) as POTUS.
Hoover was orphaned at just nine years old. By 11, he moved to Oregon to live with his uncle, and by 14, he quit school to work in his uncleâs real estate business. At age 17, he was admitted to Stanford University, where he met his future wife, Lou Henry, and studied mining engineering.
For nearly two decades after graduating, Hoover worked as an international mining engineer and financier, earning a âfortuneâ (around $4 million), according to the National Archives, while based in London. When World War I began, he organized efforts to provide food relief and help American travelers escape war zones.
In 1917, Hoover returned to the U.S., where President Woodrow Wilson appointed him to lead the new U.S. Food Administration. His effective leadership won over the American people, and in 1920, Wilson personally chose Hoover as Secretary of Commerce. In this role, Hoover raised manufacturing standards and helped promote advancements in radio and aviation. He continued under President Calvin Coolidge, fronting major relief efforts during the Mississippi River floods and earning the nickname âMaster of Emergencies,â a title that, unfortunately, would not stick with him through his four years in the Oval Office.
Thanks to his sterling reputation and government service, Hoover ran for president in 1928 and won âin a landslide.â Inspired by Theodore Roosevelt, the self-made millionaire-turned-president aimed to reform the economy and push for both social and environmental initiatives.
But in October 1929, the stock market crashed, and the Great Depression was imminent. Hoover persuaded business leaders to maintain workersâ wages, and he tried to combat panic by accelerating construction projects and creating new jobs.
While he proposed a $169 million tax cut and doubled public works spending, he did not âprovide direct federal relief to the unemployed.â Still, his efforts led to some economic improvement, at least until 1931.
Layoffs continued, and Americans grew resentful of what they considered wasteful spending and ineffective programs that didnât help the unemployed. By 1932, 10 million people were out of work. Hoover responded by establishing Federal Home Loan Banks to assist homeowners and the Reconstruction Finance Corporation to offer emergency loans to struggling businesses.
Although the economy saw decent improvement, it wasnât enough for Hoover to win reelection. The following year, Franklin D. Roosevelt took office, and Hoover left the White House, redirecting his talents as a writer, speaker, and chairman of several charities and non-profit groups.
After Roosevelt died in 1945, Hoover returned to public service, assisting President Harry S. Truman with postâWorld War II food relief efforts in Europe. He also played a large role in establishing Boys Clubs of America and funded a research campus at Stanford, his alma mater, years prior. 
Hoover went on to write more than 40 books and oversaw the creation of a âPresidential Libraryâ in his hometown of West Branch. He passed away on October 20, 1964, and was buried near his birthplace.
From Midwest orphan to self-made millionaire to President to author, Herbert Hooverâs journey was, without a doubt, one of the most unique in the history of the American presidents.Â
