Week in review Featured

Xiaomi revealed the Redmi K100 Pro and K100 Pro Max ahead of their August 11 launch. The K100 Pro gets a redesigned camera island, a 6.59-inch 185Hz display, and Qualcomm's Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 V Series chipset, while the Pro Max adds a 6.9-inch OLED display, a 9,000mAh battery, and a triple 50MP rear camera with a periscope telephoto lens. Pricing and availability will be announced at launch.
Meanwhile, OnePlus sold out its entire smartphone stock in North America, essentially marking an end to its presence in North America. 
The K100 Pro series will debut in China on August 11.
The K100 Pro series will be unveiled on August 11.
You might still find a few leftover units at third-party retailers, but the brand is officially out of the North American market.
Next up is Samsung. Rumors this week have to do with the cameras of the next-gen S models. The Galaxy S27 Pro will reportedly feature a 200MP main camera, a 50MP ultrawide, and a 12MP 3x telephoto. Previous rumors had it much closer to the Ultra 50MP 3.5x zoom, but alas.
Speaking of the Ultra, the Galaxy S27 Ultra will lose the 3x telephoto camera and retain the 200MP main, 50MP ultrawide, and 50MP 5x telephoto. Users will just have to resort to in-sensor zoom from the main 200MP camera for their 3x needs.
Samsung unveiled the Galaxy F70 Pro in India with a 6.7-inch 120Hz AMOLED display, Snapdragon 6 Gen 3 chipset, a 6,000mAh battery with 45W charging, and a triple 50MP rear camera setup. The phone is promised to receive six Android OS updates and six years of security patches, and starts at INR 24,999 for the 8/128GB model, with sales beginning on August 11.
It is rumored to offer a 200MP main rear sensor.
The dated 10MP 3x telephoto camera on the Galaxy S Ultra will finally retire next year.
It’s equipped with a Snapdragon 6 Gen 3 chipset.

Meanwhile, leaked promo materials confirmed some previously rumored features of the Pixel 11 Pro, namely the Tensor G6 SoC, up to 120x AI-enhanced digital zoom, and the new ""Pixel Glow"" notification light.

19,2026-08-09,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Thomas Flohr, the 65-year-old founder, chairman, and CEO of VistaJet, based in St. Moritz, Switzerland, about his daily routine. It's been edited for length and clarity.
I started my career at a Chicago-based technology firm in 1985 and rose through the ranks. I later acquired most of the company's European operations through my company, Comprendium Investment.
My frequent business travel in the early 2000s exposed me to the shortcomings of private aviation. At the time, I found the private jet experience to be inconsistent and underwhelming. The service quality didn't match the level of luxury that clients paying $10,000 to $15,000 an hour expected. I didn't see a good solution in the market and initially thought about paying for fractional jet ownership before deciding against it.
Business Insider's Power Hours series gives readers an inside look at how powerful leaders in business structure their workday. Reach out to editor Lauryn Haas to share your daily routine.
After exploring all other options, I bought a Learjet 60 in 2003, and that plane became the foundation for starting VistaJet in 2004. Vista is a private aviation subscription service that provides access to over 270 customized business jets for clients in 187 countries.
My primary job is building client relationships and setting the company's strategic direction. Here's what a typical day in my life looks like.
Our company is headquartered in Dubai, but I travel frequently and usually work from any of our 25 offices worldwide, including my home office in St. Moritz. I wake up at different times, depending on where I am. If I'm in America, I start earlier to sync with Europe. In the Middle East, I work late into the night to overlap with American and European business hours.
In St. Moritz, my mornings start around 8 a.m. I shower, grab coffee, and ease into the day. I don't eat breakfast.
Try a
                                Level Up action pack.
Build a calm, realistic plan to recover from job loss and move toward your next role.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
I prefer not to reach for my phone right away. About 45 minutes after my coffee, I'll respond to overnight emails and messages before diving into the day's activities.
I maintain several offices in different locations so my executives can use them when I'm not around. If I'm home, I'll work from my home office. If I'm on the road, I'll work on the plane or at my destination.
Sometimes, I work from the hotel in the morning and knock out key business tasks. If I'm in Dubai, London, New York, or a city with a VistaJet office, I'll head there in the afternoon for team and client meetings.
I often sit where the action is with the team or join executive committee members in their offices.
I prioritize client meetings before internal ones and don't believe internal meetings should take more than 20 to 30 minutes unless there's a compelling reason.
When clients call me directly, it's usually because they're about to make a significant decision, like buying a plane or finalizing a global flight plan. I also work on product development, brainstorming new services and product feature updates between calls and meetings.
As a founder, I'm still very hands-on, and I'm in constant communication with my finance and operations teams.
For lunch, I keep it very light and healthy. I usually eat vegetable soup, a fruit platter, or a light Japanese sashimi dish.
If I'm having a client lunch meeting, I prefer to eat at restaurants that use high-quality ingredients. Some of my favorite restaurants include Scott's in Mayfair, London, and Gaia in Dubai. When I eat out, I mostly get grilled seafood and boiled potatoes — nothing fancy.
The afternoon often means syncing with our US and European teams. In our business, there's no shortage of unusual requests that require creative solutions. One memorable request involved helping a client run three marathons on three continents in one week. It was a significant logistical undertaking, but we successfully pulled it off for the client.
Another request involved helping a corporate client coordinate the flight of executives from four continents to a board meeting in India, ensuring they all arrived precisely on time and returned home safely afterward.
If there's a gap between meetings, I'll take a 10-15-minute break to walk outside and get some fresh air. I try to prioritize these short breaks to clear my head and reset, but most of the time, the day runs nonstop.
By 6 or 6:30 p.m., I head to the gym and spend an hour training. Once I'm done working out, I hit the steam room for 15 to 20 minutes to relax and recover. Staying fit keeps me sharp.
I'm a firm believer in an integrated life. I don't view work and life as separate entities that need to be balanced against each other. Instead, I see everything in life as one endurance effort, whether in business or sports like car racing.
The time I eat dinner varies depending on my location. In some countries, I eat at 7 p.m.; in others, at 9 or 9:30 p.m. I adapt to the time zone and cultural norms. When I'm home, I eat dinner around 8:30 p.m., but I'm always available for client calls if needed.
I enjoy cooking and dining with friends and family. I have a bowling alley in my home, so we head downstairs for a few rounds after dinner. It's a great way to unwind and stir up friendly competition.
With so much digital stimulation during the day, I avoid screens at night. I don't watch TV, but I listen to classical music or rock from the late '70s and early '80s before bed.
I usually fall asleep around 11:30 p.m., sleeping seven hours on weeknights and nine on weekends.
Despite flying around 500 hours annually, I make time for things I enjoy. I'm a big fan of simple living and doing things in nature, like walking, hiking, and shopping at my local market for fruits and vegetables.

Microsoft is finally addressing Windows 11’s clutter, performance issues, and inconsistent design — but the cleanup comes after years of user frustration.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

 



Windows 11 is finally starting to see the changes Microsoft has promised for months, except the best parts are still limited to Windows Insiders and mockups. I appreciate Microsoft's efforts to revamp Windows 11 — better late than never — but some of these improvements should have shipped years ago.
The status of Windows 11 makes me ask why Microsoft didn't aggressively start climbing the metaphorical mountain years ago. Microsoft has revamped settings and UI elements, but the big changes are part of a new initiative that honestly shouldn't need to exist.
Microsoft is updating several core parts of Windows 11 with WinUI, which improves performance and even helps Windows 11 run on PCs with less RAM. But I can't help but ask how we got here in the first place. How did we end up with a ""modern"" OS with legacy components and UI elements from different decades?
Microsoft's CVP of Design and Research on Windows, Marcus Ash, sits down with Windows Central to talk all things UI on Windows 11.
Marcus Ash, Microsoft's design and research lead for Windows 11, sits down to talk about how the company is planning to optimize Windows 11 for devices with low amounts of RAM.
Everything from the Start menu to legacy File Explorer dialogs are being rewritten with WinUI in the coming months.
Windows Insiders need to update to a build with a renewed flight certificate before August 11, 2026 to prevent builds from expiring and to keep automatic updates flowing.
Creators were able to connect an NVIDIA RTX 4060 to an ASUS Zenbook A16 with a Snapdragon X2 Elite Extreme. Is this a glimmer of hope for Windows on Arm gaming?
Reports indicate a BIOS update is bricking some Lenovo Legion Go gaming handhelds. Subreddit mods warn users to avoid the update as out-of-warranty repairs cost over $270.
I understand that updating legacy components can be tricky. Microsoft's CVP of Design and Research on Windows, Marcus Ash, joined the most recent Windows Central Podcast and discussed the challenges of updating Windows.
Obviously, Ash knows more about Windows than I ever will. I don't question the difficulty of updating an entire OS, especially if you need to re-update components several times.
What I question is why keeping Windows up to date and unified was not a core value from the start? Why did Microsoft reach a point where user feedback became overwhelming and the tech giant finally decided to make bigger changes?
Legacy support is critical for enterprise users, so Microsoft could not just throw out old code or elements to make Windows modern. But having a unified OS could have been a priority from the beginning.
Work like this needed to start decades ago, not in 2026. Some of the groundwork has been laid for these improvements, but some projects are quite new. That's not an acceptable way to make an operating system.
My top recommendation isn't an item on sale. Instead, it's a must-have for Xbox owners that's running out of stock. Several retailers have listed the Seagate Storage Expansion Card for Xbox Series X|S as unavailable. Best Buy ran out of stock while I was writing a piece highlighting the shortage. 22,2026-08-09,"Uncrewed aircraft are becoming more important to militaries, and defense departments are increasingly looking to air-taxi companies for help.
Beta Technologies unveiled the MV250, an autonomous hybrid-electric cargo aircraft, at the Farnborough Airshow in July. Business Insider was there to see why the company believes it could be a valuable tool for the future battlefield.
Beta initially focused on civil electric aircraft. But as militaries seek new ways to move supplies amid increasingly autonomous warfare, Beta has adapted its technology for a new customer.
The MV250 can take off and land vertically, fly without a pilot aboard, and carry up to a ton of cargo into dangerous areas.
Here's what it's like, and why it matters.
These eVTOLs operate like helicopters but make very little noise. So, they wouldn't annoy residents when flying over cities.
Beta is also building an electric aircraft that takes off and lands conventionally, known as a CTOL.
Earlier this year, Beta deployed its all-electric ALIA CTOL aircraft during NATO Exercise Aurora 26 in Sweden.
It is a hybrid-electric aircraft that can take off vertically and fly autonomously.
""Why do we all want autonomy? I'm a pilot. No pilot wants to see their job go away,"" said Gen. Jim McConville, a former chief of staff of the US Army and a Beta board member.
""But what we want to do is give commanders the ability to go into situations at extremely high risk.""
For example, in Ukraine, aerial drones and ground robots are increasingly being used for logistics missions that have become too dangerous for soldiers.
Ukrainian officials have said that they want machines to eventually handle 100% of battlefield logistics amid a broader push to replace humans with robotics wherever possible.
The MV250's nose opens to reveal a cargo bay capable of carrying up to 2,000 pounds of equipment, medical supplies, munitions, or other critical cargo.
Beta says it can take that payload to destinations within a 250-nautical-mile tactical radius, roughly 288 miles, and return to base.
A turbogenerator developed with GE Aerospace produces electricity for the MV250's motors.
As well as the 1,300-nautical-mile repositioning range — when it isn't carrying a payload — Beta says it can generate power on the ground for equipment such as radar, antennas, and other vehicles.
The MV250's fuselage is bonded together rather than held with conventional fasteners.
""That allows us to make this in a panini press, basically,"" Clark said. ""You can make it inexpensively and meet the demands of the military.""
Beta says manufacturing cycle times could be measured in hours rather than months.
It has also designed the aircraft without gearboxes, hydraulics, or articulating rotors, systems that can add maintenance demands.
During earlier military exercises with Beta's all-electric aircraft, Clark said the company sent one maintainer and one pilot — and ""the maintainer was bored.""
Beta developed the technology behind the MV250 for civilian electric aircraft, but Clark said defense customers may adopt vertical-lift technology sooner.
""Near-term defense, I think, will take a pace in front of civil in vertical lift, and then civil will overtake that,"" he said.
Defense companies made up about half of the roughly 1,600 exhibitors at this year's airshow, up from the roughly 40% share they usually represent. The event drew over 140,000 visitors, a 41% increase on the last edition in 2024.
The shift reflects a scramble to rearm as wars have reshaped military priorities, including the need for cheaper drones, autonomous systems, and new ways to move supplies.
NATO allies have also committed to raising defense and security-related spending to 5% of GDP by 2035, after President Donald Trump pressed them to spend more.

23,2026-08-09,"Google’s Pixel Launcher is one of the easiest and most flexible launchers to use. I don’t need to tweak heavy settings or adjust endless toggles to keep it running out of the box.
When it comes to app notifications, though, leaving these settings untouched can drown you in alerts, even with the new notification manager tools, especially if you have dozens installed (or over a hundred).
After over a year of daily driving my Google Pixel 9 Pro XL, I found which settings and features to flip on to avoid getting swamped by pesky alerts while stopping me from missing the crucial ones.
I should have done this sooner
 If you’re like me and installed close to or over a hundred apps on your Pixel, you know how quickly app notifications can swarm your notification shade, such as when you wake up in the morning after silencing them overnight.
Google released Notification organizer to manage this problem I and others have faced.
It shipped with Android 16 QPR at the end of last year and supports newer Pixel models like the Pixel 9 and Pixel 10 that have on-device AI.
It works by automatically grouping different notifications and silencing them. They still appear in the notification bar and drawer, but each notification window stays collapsed.
Notifications from multiple apps are arranged per app, so you still get multiple minimized items within each grouping.
It’s surprisingly effective at keeping me focused on more important alerts, prioritizing them over low-priority items like promotional ads and social media pushes that I find annoying.
Depending on your device, Notification organizer might be switched off by default.
Accessing and turning it on is a straightforward process: Go to your Pixel settings and tap Notifications. Select Notification organizer and turn it on.
Alternatively, you can access each Notification organizer toggle from an app’s notification page.
I also recommend tweaking the other controls inside the feature, such as toggling on Social and Suggested in addition to News and Promotion.
You can customize which apps will be excluded from Notification organizer, which gives you neat granular control. For example, I keep the toggle active for finance apps as well as YouTube Music and Slack.
I left Notification summaries sitting untouched for months before realizing how effective it is for managing long chat threads or messages.
It’s the equivalent of Notification organizer, but built for rambling conversations. And because it relies on on-device artificial intelligence, it’s only available on newer Pixel generations.
When turned on, it summarizes and groups chats via messaging or social media apps. The result is a compact window with summarized chat entries rather than individual conversations filling up your notification drawer.
I found that it’s not overly aggressive, either. I’ve noticed it only kicks in when threads are ridiculously long.
Even so, you can customize which apps are included in Notification summaries, so Google won’t force it across the board.
I keep WhatsApp and Messages exempted from summarization so that I don’t miss important messages from family and friends.
Even on older Pixel models – and generally on devices running stock Android – there’s a classic tool for managing apps and filtering out the noise.
It’s labeled Notification categories on stock Android, while on Samsung Galaxy devices running One UI, you’ll find it under Manage notification categories for each app in the advanced settings.
On Pixels, it’s enabled by default and works out of the box. However, the feature doesn’t automatically apply to every app or newly installed app.
Leaving it alone often leaves crucial toggles off, so it’s worth checking it individually.
It’s as vital as the other notification tools on Android, allowing me to customize which types of alerts are filtered out.
For instance, I can choose to receive only direct messages from Instagram while shutting off promotions and push alerts.
The only drawback is that you have to check each app’s settings page. But after you’ve dialed everything in, you won’t need to touch it again.
Another classic tool that leans more toward accessibility is Flash notifications.
It features two modes: camera flash and screen flash. Older devices with a dedicated LED notification light support similar visual triggers.
It uses visual cues through the camera flash or display when an incoming notification hits or an alarm is running.
On other devices, you can also customize the frequency or intensity of the camera flash, as well as the color of the screen flash, such as on my Pixel phone.
But there’s a missing control I wish were added. There’s no way to select specific apps to trigger the screen flash, which would make a world of difference for priority alerts. But having the option is better than nothing.
It’s one more way to guarantee I won’t miss a ping when my phone is sitting silent on my desk.
Are you ignoring these Pixel settings?
 I’m glad Google is taking notification overload seriously with these new controls, even if the execution isn’t entirely foolproof.
With a few quick settings adjustments on my Pixel phone, I can focus on important alerts instead of feeling like my phone is constantly poking at me.
I hope we get more control in the future, though it’s not really a significant constraint. What’s already available is enough to control pesky alerts while also letting me decide what stays.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

		Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.	
Wellness > Fitness
These are the products I actually use and recommend for active individuals.
Fitness and Nutrition Writer
Fitness and Nutrition Writer
August 9, 2026, 10:00 am ET
The Cross Training Peloton Bike Plus is my go-to if you’re a Peloton fan.
Using the right fitness and wellness tech can help you to achieve your goals and also help with recovery. As CNET’s resident fitness expert, I’ve tested everything from the best treadmills to smart rings and smart home gyms. As great as some of these products are, there are a few I keep coming back to — and recommending to friends and family. If it’s in your budget and you’re looking to upgrade or improve your workout routine, there are some gadgets I recommend investing in. These are the pieces of tech that you may want to consider to step up your workout and recovery game.
Don’t miss any of our unbiased tech content and lab-based reviews. Add CNET as a preferred Google source on Chrome.
The Normatec Go compression boots are TSA-friendly and compact enough to fit in a backpack.
The Hyperice Normatec Go compression boots are ideal if you want to recover on the go after a tough workout. These are TSA-friendly so you can travel with them in a carry-on bag or in your gym bag. As a petite woman, the standard Normatec legs tend to be too big on me, so I like that these compression boots are short enough to target the calves. I’ve found it helpful to use them after a run and tough leg day. These are also easily rechargeable and have a 3-hour battery life. 
The Apple Watch Series 11 is the newest Apple Watch to hit the market and it has some unique upgrades.
Although I haven’t had a chance to test the Apple Watch Series 11, I am familiar with the brand and am a loyal Apple Watch owner. The latest version of the Apple Watch has upgraded features like FDA-cleared hypertension alerts, a longer battery life, sleep score and a more scratch-resistant screen. CNET Lead Writer Vanessa Hand Orellana reviewed the smartwatch and said the Series 11 felt like picking up where she left off from the last Apple Watch iteration. Although the Apple Watch already tracked sleep, this is the first time you’ll get quantifiable data that follows clinical guidance to grade your quality of sleep based on sleep duration, bedtime and interruptions. 
The stand-out health feature on the Series 11 is the new FDA-cleared hypertension notifications. It tracks your blood pressure and alerts you to potential irregularities. You should double-check this data with a proper blood pressure monitor and your doctor, because the Apple Watch reading isn’t meant to be used as a diagnostic tool. 
Another important aspect to highlight is the longer battery life, which helps you enjoy your phone’s features for multiple days before needing to recharge it. This is a great option if you’re looking to upgrade an older Apple Watch model or are new to smartwatches. 
The Theragun Mini 3 is tiny yet powerful, and it is a favorite of mine if you want a compact massage gun that packs a mighty punch. Massage guns are helpful to have on hand when you have sore muscles. However, most are on the larger side, so a smaller option is always a good idea. 
The Theragun Mini 3 is quieter and lighter than its previous versions and comes with three massage heads (standard ball, dampener and thumb attachments) and a soft carrying case. It’s TSA-friendly and small enough to fit in your purse or backpack. Other factors that make it appealing are that it has three settings and even at its lowest setting, it’s still a powerful device. You can even pair it with the Therabody app to give you recovery plans to follow. You can’t go wrong with a Theragun Mini, whether you’re looking for one for yourself or as a gift for a friend or family member.
The Theragun PRO Plus is Therabody’s most powerful massage gun and it’s loaded with features.
On the other hand, if you’re an athlete putting yourself through tough workouts, the Theragun PRO Plus will quickly become your favorite recovery tool. The Theragun PRO Plus is designed with serious athletes in mind, with various customizable and preset programs, including near-infrared LED light, hot and cold therapy to soothe sore muscles. This heavy-duty massage gun is quieter than the previous model (Theragun PRO-5th Generation) and comes with six attachments, so you’ll get the right angle each time. Additionally, if you want to use the cold therapy function, you’ll need to purchase the PRO Plus cold attachment separately. 
If you love to work out hard and take recovery seriously, this massage gun is the best gift you can buy yourself.
The Oura Ring 5 is the closest a smart ring has come to looking like jewelry on my finger.
If you ask me, the Oura Ring is still the best smart ring on the market. It’s most recent version, the Oura Ring 5 quickly won me over because it’s 40% smaller and lighter than the Oura Ring 4. The smart ring tracks your overall health in a holistic manner by gathering your stress levels, heart rate, cycle insights if you menstruate and your sleep. This ring is best known for its ability to track your sleep habits and provide insight to help you improve them based on that data.
The design itself is scratch-resistant, which is a step up from previous models because they would easily get scuffed up. Oura’s original symptom radar, which could detect if you were getting sick, has been updated to be called Health Radar and can detect blood pressure trends. Additionally, if you like to work out, the ring has an automatic workout detection function and can record your workouts.  It takes about a month to get the most accurate results, and from there, Oura gives you a readiness score, which tells you how recovered you are, based on sleep and stress levels, to take on the day. 
Although it isn’t necessary to own a smart wearable, it’s sometimes helpful to know details about your day-to-day habits that you may be unaware of or how they may be affecting you. For example, as a mom to a toddler, I don’t always get restful sleep, and the Oura Ring was able to detect that and support me as I adjusted my sleep schedule based on its recommendations.
The Tonal 2 replaces all your strength training equipment with this one device. 
If you’re serious about strength training and have the budget and space in your home, you’ll love owning the Tonal 2. The Tonal 2 is a smart home gym that uses up to 250 pounds of resistance and is designed to replace dumbbells, barbells and other strength-training equipment. It resembles a cable machine with a screen in the middle and adjustable arms on each side. 
Tonal 2 uses AI through 13 sensors to track your form and technique, which helps you correct it. Accessories like the smart handles and bar accessories (an optional, additional $495) have a gyroscope motion sensor that also keeps track of your reps. I’ve tested both the Tonal 2 and its predecessor, The Tonal, and was impressed by the thought process that went into designing this machine. It offers strength training as well as some cardio with its new Aero feature, keeps track of your workouts, functions like a personal trainer because it can determine if you need to scale it back or challenge yourself, and also offers thousands of on-demand and live workouts for all fitness levels. The price tag is hefty at $4,295, but if you’re dedicated to strength training and want everything you need at your fingertips, this is the machine for you.
I tested the Peloton Cross-Training Bike Plus along with other exercise bikes. 
The smart bike that took the fitness world by storm during the COVID-19 pandemic is popular for good reason. I’ve tested many exercise bikes — including Peloton alternatives — and the Peloton is still the best option. In 2025, Peloton overhauled its equipment and introduced its Cross Training Plus line. This included the new Peloton Cross Training Bike Plus, which replaced the Peloton Bike Plus. The latest version of the bike comes with a movement-tracking camera and Peloton IQ — Peloton’s AI coach — which can count reps, correct your form and identify when to increase weights during a strength-training class. Other cosmetic updates include a new seat, a three-speed fan and a phone holder.
Peloton delivers and assembles the bike in your home and sets up your account, so you don’t have to do much but hop on the bike and ride. You can connect with other Peloton members and have friendly competition on the leaderboard. The instructors are high-energy, and each offers something different and motivational to their classes. I’m personally partial to Alex Toussaint and Robin Arzon because every time I take their classes, I’m always inspired to push myself more. Not to mention, Peloton is always revitalizing its workout program offerings (like with kettlebell classes) and challenges, so you won’t ever get bored.
The Beats Powerbeats Pro 2 offer excellent audio and noise-canceling features, so you can enjoy music while doing various activities.
I take listening to my music seriously during my workouts. The Beats Powerbeats Pro 2 are by far some of my favorite earbuds to wear when breaking a sweat. The battery life is impressive and, in my experience, can last for weeks at a time before needing to be recharged. This can vary as it also depends on how often you use them. 
The noise-canceling aspect of the Powerbeats Pro 2 is impressive, so your music will always feel immersive whether you’re at the beginning, middle or end of your workout. I’ve also been able to receive phone calls while wearing them, and never had connection issues. I’ve used these earbuds during strength training sessions, outdoor and indoor runs and my daily walks. If you’re looking for a pair of earbuds that stay put (thanks to the ear hook design), have crisp audio and are stylish, these are my top pick.

						Best new wireless sports earbuds with ear hooks					
The Future Pro app connects you with a personal trainer right in your home.
As a fitness writer, I’ve reviewed a bunch of fitness apps, but one that I particularly enjoyed is the Future Pro app. It’s the ideal app if you’re looking for a virtual personal trainer who can create a workout program for you based on your fitness level and whether you work out at home or the gym. 
The app costs $50 a month to start and $199 thereafter. When you sign up, you fill out an assessment intake form, which basically asks you about your goals, workout history, how often you’d like to work out, and what motivates you. Based on your responses, the form offers a selection of trainers to choose from. 
I used the Future Pro app for more than a year and loved how well-thought-out and seamless it is. I was paired with a trainer whom I enjoyed checking in with daily through the app chat portal. This app got me through my pregnancy and postpartum period, so I could resume exercising safely. The workouts for the week are shared at the top of the week, and if you need any modifications or if you plan on taking a vacation, the trainer can modify your schedule as needed. The workouts are easy to follow, and the exercises are accompanied by video demonstrations in case you need them for reference. If you want a personal trainer at your fingertips, this is the app to download on your phone.

27,2026-08-09,"Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman goes into some detail today about the future for the Apple Watch product line. 
He suggests Apple is going back to the drawing board as the Watch faces stiffer competition from the likes of the Oura Ring and other screenless smart fitness devices. It seems Apple is preparing to revamp the concept of the Apple Watch from the ground up …
Although it sounds like plans haven’t been finalized, Apple is considering all sorts of shapes and sizes, including watches with circular round displays, fitness accessories that have no screens at all, and more. This would represent a major breakpoint in the history of the Apple Watch, as every model up to now has basically revolved around the same basic design concept.
Bloomberg indicates Apple is looking at possibilities on the low-end and the high-end, weighing up the appeal of both more premium and more affordable models. The new wearable form factors would also intersect with a software revamp, in which Apple is looking to push the Health app in a more AI-centric direction.
However, nothing is close to shipping. It doesn’t even sound like Apple has decided on something concretely as of yet. So anything radically new is still years away from coming to market.
This fall, Apple will release new iterative models of the Watch, including Apple Watch Series 12 and Ultra 4. These will feature new CPUs, some new color options and improved fitness features. 
The new Watches will probably launch at the company’s annual September event, which will also be the venue for the unveiling of the new iPhone 18 Pro and the first foldable iPhone, potentially branded as ‘iPhone Ultra’.

29,2026-08-09,"Earlier this summer, Apple dramatically increased its prices as a result of the ongoing memory crisis around the globe. Things like iPad Air increased from $599 to $749, M5 MacBook Pro from $1699 to $1999, and Apple TV from $129 to $199. That’s just a few examples, but the point is, these price hikes are steep.
It leaves the question – will Apple work on major improvements to make its products more appealing at these new prices?
Of course, some of Apple’s products have impending overhauls. MacBook Pro and iPad mini are two of them. For products that don’t have anything major in the pipeline though – I can’t imagine Apple will be super successful in selling yesterday’s tech at much higher prices and still have people feeling super compelled – especially compared to the current state of the used market.
If you could buy an M4 iPad Air for $599 (or as low as $499) a few months ago – why would you want to pay $749 now? The argument scales even further with higher priced products.
With some of Apple’s products, they haven’t changed much in quite some time. iPad Air has kept the same design and feature set since 2020 – only gaining chip upgrades and Apple Pencil Pro since. The base iPad has remained largely the same since 2022, iMac the same since 2021, and so on.
Do these products need redesigns? Not necessarily. However, I think Apple could do things to make these products more enticing at their existing price points. Perhaps miniLED displays on the iMac, 120Hz on the iPad Air, or a laminated display on the base iPad. 
Those would be relatively cheap upgrades compared to the increased prices – and would probably help users feel a lot better about buying at these newer prices. On the flip side, Apple could potentially downgrade some products to offset the higher prices. Perhaps we’ll see a return of 128GB iPhones.
How do you feel about Apple’s product lineup post-price hikes? Let us know in the comments.

30,2026-08-09,"Sometime in 1977, a 35mm single-lens reflex interrupted your evening television. A Japanese company had bought national network airtime to sell it, which almost no camera maker had seriously tried, and the pitch was not about sharpness or lens quality. It was that the thing was easy.
The camera was the Canon AE-1, and it is worth being honest about what it was and what it was not. It was not the finest SLR you could buy in 1976. It was not the most rugged, and it was not the choice of most working professionals, who mostly stayed with their all-mechanical bodies. What the AE-1 did was different and, in the long run, bigger. It took the SLR, an instrument that had always assumed you knew what you were doing, and it handed it to people who did not. It did that through a small piece of silicon, a single automatic mode, and an advertising budget that treated a camera like a car.
Start with the engineering, because everything else follows from it. The AE-1 was, by Canon's own account, the first 35mm SLR built around a central processing unit. A microcomputer sat at the center of the camera and handled the timing and the exposure math that older cameras did with springs, gears, and hand-assembled linkages.
That decision was not really about giving you features. It was about cost. When a chip controls timing, you no longer need dozens of precision metal parts to do the same job mechanically. Canon redesigned the whole body around this idea, organizing it into five major units and twenty-five minor units that could be assembled and adjusted automatically. By Canon's own museum count, moving those functions into electronics let the company cut the parts count by about 300.
Fewer parts meant something specific on the factory floor. Modular construction let Canon build the AE-1 on highly automated production lines instead of relying on the slow, skilled hand-assembly that a mechanical camera demands. The top panel was injection-molded plastic, an acrylonitrile-butadiene-styrene shell finished to look like metal, which trimmed both weight and cost. Purists noticed. The AE-1 was less impact resistant than a brass-and-steel body, and it felt it. But it also cost far less to make, and that saving was the entire point.
There was a real trade-off baked into all of this. A camera run by a chip needs power, so the AE-1 leaned on its battery in a way older SLRs never did. A fully mechanical body would fire at every shutter speed with a dead cell, but let the AE-1 go flat and it mostly stopped working. That dependence bothered photographers who had grown up trusting a camera to run on nothing. It was also the direction the entire industry was about to go, and the AE-1 was the body that made most people stop worrying about it.
This is the part people miss when they call the AE-1 revolutionary. The revolution was not a headline spec. It was a manufacturing strategy dressed up as a camera. Canon figured out that if it built a good-enough SLR cheaply and consistently enough, it could sell it to a mass market few makers had seriously chased: ordinary people who wanted a real camera and had been priced and intimidated out of one.
The chip enabled the feature that did the actual selling. The AE-1 offered shutter-priority automatic exposure, and if you have only ever shot digital, it is easy to underrate how much that mattered in 1976.
Here is what it meant in practice. You picked a shutter speed. You focused. The camera read the light through the lens and chose the aperture for you, and it did this the instant you pressed the shutter button. You did not have to understand the relationship between aperture and shutter speed. You did not have to balance a needle in the viewfinder or interpret a match diode and then translate it into a decision. You turned one dial and pressed the button.
Think about who that was for. Not the hobbyist who already loved the craft of metering, though he could still switch to full manual and work the camera the old way. Shutter priority was aimed squarely at the person standing next to him: the parent, the traveler, the student, the person who wanted sharp, correctly exposed pictures of their own life and did not want a physics lesson to get them. The AE-1 let that person buy in without first earning the knowledge. Shutter priority in particular flattered the way beginners actually think, because most people intuitively grasp ""freeze the action"" or ""let it blur"" long before they understand what f/11 does to depth of field.
The rest of the camera was competent rather than exotic. Shutter speeds ran from 2 seconds to 1/1000 second. It used Canon's FD breech-lock mount, which opened the door to a large and affordable lineup of lenses, so the cheap body was the start of a system you could actually grow into. A photographer who outgrew the kit lens could add a fast prime like the Canon FD 50mm f/1.8 without changing anything else. None of this was cutting edge on its own. Assembled around that microcomputer and sold at that price, it was something no rival had put together in quite the same way.
A cheap, easy SLR still has to reach the people who do not read camera magazines, and this is where Canon did the thing it is really remembered for. The company put the AE-1 on national American television.
That sounds unremarkable now. It was close to unheard of then. Camera advertising lived in enthusiast print, where you spoke to people already fluent in apertures and lens tests. Buying network airtime to sell a 35mm SLR to a general audience was a genuine gamble, and by most accounts Canon was among the first camera makers, and the first Japanese one, to bet on national US TV to move an SLR. The spots leaned on athletes, most famously the tennis champion John Newcombe, and drove home a single promise. The tagline was ""So advanced, it's simple."" One version stretched it to ""The surprisingly affordable Canon AE-1: so advanced, it's simple,"" which tells you exactly which two objections Canon set out to kill.

The scale of the spend became part of the legend. It is often repeated that Canon spent about as much marketing the AE-1 as it did designing and building it, though that figure is best treated as an oft-cited claim rather than an audited number. The message never changed across any of it. You did not need to be a photographer to use this. You just had to buy it.
The pricing history is genuinely muddy, so be careful with any single number you see quoted. Reports from the period put the AE-1 body anywhere from around $200 to roughly $300 depending on the year, the retailer, the finish, and whether a lens came in the box, with discount houses undercutting list. What is not in dispute is the relative position. The AE-1 landed noticeably below its most obvious rivals while offering automation few of them could match at that price, and that gap is what the commercials were built to exploit.
The strategy worked to a degree that is still slightly hard to believe. Canon sold past a million AE-1 bodies quickly enough to call it unprecedented for an SLR, and unofficial estimates based on serial numbers put total production at around 5.7 million over the camera's 1976 to 1984 run. By 1981 an industry analyst was calling the AE-1 the Chevrolet of the 35mm market, which was not a compliment about prestige and was entirely a statement about volume.
Numbers that large change the thing they describe. A generation of people learned photography on an AE-1 because it was the camera in the house, the one a parent bought because a tennis player on TV said it was simple. Plenty of them started in shutter-priority mode, got curious, flipped to manual, and taught themselves the craft the camera had let them skip at first. The AE-1 became a gateway, and Canon knew it, following up with the AE-1 Program in 1981 to catch the buyer who wanted even the shutter speed chosen for them.
The deeper legacy is that the AE-1 normalized the ideas that now define every camera you own. Automated exposure as the default. Electronics at the core instead of the edges. A camera designed to be manufactured cheaply and sold to everyone rather than machined slowly for specialists. Every rival had to answer it, and the industry spent the next decade racing toward more automation and more electronics because Canon had proven the mass market was real and would pay for ease.
Pick up an AE-1 today and it still makes the case for itself. It is one of the most common ways a new film shooter starts, because the used bodies are everywhere, the FD glass is cheap, and the camera does exactly what it promised a tennis player would do for you in 1977. It gets out of your way. That was never an accident, and it was never really about being the best. It was about being the one that finally let you in.

31,2026-08-09,"Most warnings about the next few decades reach for a single dramatic hinge â a collapse, a war, a tipping point with a date attached. The more uncomfortable possibility is that there isn't one hinge. Three systems that have nothing to do with each other â the atmosphere, human reproduction, and the architecture of state power â are all under strain at once, on different clocks, for different reasons, and nobody is coordinating the response. That's a harder story to tell than a single crisis, but it's the one the data actually supports.
The baseline fact is not in dispute. Atmospheric CO2 averaged about 280 ppm for the roughly ten thousand years before the Industrial Revolution began around 1760, a level NOAA treats as the reference point for ""pre-industrial"" precisely because it matches high-resolution ice-core measurements from Law Dome, Antarctica, covering the years 1000 to 1800. That baseline held for most of recorded human civilization. It no longer holds. NOAA's Mauna Loa observatory recorded a fresh record of 432 ppm in May 2026 â and the more important number isn't the peak itself, but the pace: CO2 is now accumulating roughly three times faster than it did in the 1960s. The UK Met Office's seasonal forecast puts the full-year 2026 average at around 429.4 ppm, continuing an unbroken rise across nearly seven decades of direct measurement.
What makes this worth dwelling on isn't the number â it's the denominator. Biological adaptation of the kind that reshapes a species' physiology or ecological range typically unfolds across thousands of generations. This shift has unfolded across one human lifetime.
The IPCC's Working Group II assessment frames the consequence in exactly the right terms â not a single global endpoint, but adaptation limits being exceeded unevenly, in specific regions and specific systems, at different times. Ecosystems aren't vanishing so much as being asked to reorganize on a timeline nothing evolved to handle. That reorganization is slower and messier than ""collapse,"" but no less real for being gradual.
The second pressure has nothing to do with carbon and would be happening in a stable climate too. East Asia's fertility collapse is real, documented, and arguably under-covered outside the region. South Korea's total fertility rate bottomed at 0.72 in 2023, and has since recovered only to 0.80 in 2025 â still a fraction of the roughly 2.1 births per woman needed to hold population steady, and Seoul's own rate, at 0.63, remains the lowest of any major city in the country.
But the demographic collapse is a downstream symptom, not the root cause â and the root cause is where the numbers stop being clean and start being human. One of the starkest features of Korea's marriage decline is a widening gender divide: across three decades, men have consistently been less likely than women to marry, and adjusting for delayed marriage timing changes little â fewer people are marrying at all, not just marrying later. That retreat has hardened into something closer to open antagonism than quiet disengagement. Research from the Carnegie Endowment finds that simply telling unmarried men their marriage prospects are shrinking measurably increases their resistance to gender-equality policies and hardens antagonism toward women â a feedback loop where economic anxiety curdles into gendered blame, which in turn makes both marriage and parenthood less appealing to the women on the other side of that negotiation. This isn't confined to online forums: young men and women in South Korea have split so sharply along political lines that the 2022 presidential election saw close to three in five young men back one candidate while a similar share of young women backed the other â voting behavior that reflects, and reinforces, a generation increasingly unable to see the other side as a partner rather than an adversary. Layered on top of that is a quieter, less confrontational form of withdrawal: a growing cohort of Korean women who have simply opted out of the marriage market altogether, less as protest than as a considered rejection of a domestic bargain they no longer see a reason to accept.
None of this is a demographic apocalypse. It is, more plainly, evidence that the institutions meant to absorb future shocks â pension systems, care infrastructure, labor markets â are being asked to do more with a workforce that is smaller, older, and already exhausted by a domestic culture war before any climate-driven disruption compounds it. China's record graduate output is colliding with a shortage of matching jobs, while Vietnam faces the inverse mismatch of AI-relevant labor demand outpacing its education system. China's gig workforce is projected to reach 320 million people in 2026, and in the United States, the 4.2% headline unemployment rate is masking a labor market that feels considerably worse once underemployment is counted. The common thread isn't collapse â it's exhaustion, arriving early.
It's tempting to reach for a tidy synthesis: humanity, sensing the walls closing in, is pouring capital into artificial intelligence as a deliberate technological escape hatch. That story is more flattering than accurate. What's happening is a competitive, uneven capital race between a handful of firms and states, under real infrastructure and energy constraints â not a coordinated bet with a shared objective.
The market's own pricing behavior makes the point. Google Cloud grew 63% in a recent period while Meta's comparable numbers fell â investors are already pricing AI strategies differently by execution, not rewarding sector exposure indiscriminately. That's the opposite of a unified societal project; it's ordinary competitive sorting, happening inside a sector everyone has agreed to call existentially important. Money is not the same as physical capacity, and treating the AI investment surge as evidence of a coordinated human response to planetary strain confuses a market phenomenon for an intentional one.
The most defensible version of the ""state consolidation"" thesis is narrow: when critical infrastructure â desalination, closed-loop agriculture, grid-scale power â requires capital and coordination only large institutions can marshal, power tends to accumulate around whoever controls that infrastructure, not around whoever holds the most territory. But this isn't an abstraction. It's already being attempted, at a scale that shows both the appeal and the cost of the idea.
Saudi Arabia's NEOM project, and its centerpiece ""The Line,"" is the clearest real-world test case of what full technological enclosure actually looks like: two mirrored walls 500 meters tall running 170 kilometers through the desert, a fully climate-controlled, vertically stacked city with no cars, powered entirely by renewable energy and hydrogen, designed to house population densities six times greater than Manila. It is precisely the kind of closed-loop, artificial habitat the ""humans will wall themselves in rather than adapt outward"" thesis predicts â engineered air, engineered water, engineered climate, purpose-built for a hotter, more resource-constrained future. And it is also, as of 2026, a cautionary tale rather than a proof of concept: construction was suspended in September 2025 after only 2.4 of the planned 170 kilometers of foundation work were completed, following an $8 billion sovereign wealth fund write-down and internal cost projections reaching roughly $8.8 trillion â a figure that dwarfs the economy meant to fund it. The vision of humanity retreating into engineered enclosures is not science fiction; it is being tried, right now, by a state with essentially unlimited capital, and it is running headlong into the same physical and financial limits every other megaproject eventually hits.
That's the honest shape of the ""concentration"" thesis: not an inevitability, but an expensive, uncertain bet that the states and firms with the deepest balance sheets are already placing â and, so far, struggling to close out. It's worth resisting the pull to treat consolidation as the only branch on the tree. Only 34 economies have escaped the middle-income trap since the 1990s â a reminder that states can just as easily get stuck in a permanent middle tier, neither consolidating power nor collapsing, as they can ascend into the small club of infrastructure-controlling super-states.
This diagram traces the mechanism, not a prediction of outcome â it maps how two independently-caused pressures converge on a resource-and-infrastructure bottleneck. It says nothing about who wins that competition.
There's a smaller, stranger data point worth sitting with, because it captures this problem's shape better than any grand thesis could: silicone pollutants from car exhaust have now been detected globally, an industrial byproduct nobody was tracking until it showed up nearly everywhere at once. That's what slow-moving systemic pressure actually looks like â not a single crisis with a countdown, but the quiet accumulation of consequences from decisions made for entirely separate reasons, discovered later than anyone would like.
The honest claim available here is modest by design: an atmosphere moving on a decade-scale clock, a demographic and psychological exhaustion moving on a generational one, and a capital-and-infrastructure race that keeps hitting physical and financial ceilings even when the money is nearly unlimited. None of the three needed the others to happen, and none of them resolves into a single villain or a single fix. What they share is that the institutions built to absorb one shock at a time are now being asked to absorb several â and the record so far, from Seoul's marriage strike to NEOM's stalled foundations, suggests that absorption is happening more slowly, and more unevenly, than either the optimists or the doomers expect.

Juan Cole 08/09/2026 
Ann Arbor (Informed Comment) –  The ongoing conflict between the Trump administration and Iran, which Israel and the US jointly attacked out of the blue on February 28, raises the specter that the Hormuz crisis will continue into 2027.
Here is why that prospect is a scary one.
Europe does not have its usual stockpiles of diesel and fossil gas by this time of year, late summer, according to Goldman Sachs analyst  Samantha Dart .  Fossil gas is used for heating buildings, so it could be a cold winter in Europe, recalling the crisis of 2022 when the Russian war on Ukraine had first begun.  
Diesel is used to power trucks, which in turn deliver food to the supermarket and all sorts of other goods from wholesalers to retailers. Not enough diesel would lead to big shortages in food and other supplies. The fertilizer crisis because of the blockade of Hormuz will also send food prices shooting up this winter. Dart says that these possible shortfalls keep her up at night. Diesel also is used in combines and other agricultural machinery, in construction equipment and in shipping.
The International Trade Center crunched the numbers and found that in April, for instance, exports of Liquefied Natural Gas from the Gulf  fell by an astonishing 95%.  Qatar is a major supplier of LNG, accounting for about a 5th of such exports globally. For long stretches this spring and summer, Qatari gas tankers could not transit the Strait of Hormuz.  Such tankers now appear to be paying tolls to Iran and so the number of transits has increased, but exports are substantially lower than before the war.  In addition, Iranian drones took out 17% of Qatar’s LNG production capacity at Ras Laffan.
Diesel prices are high because of the crisis, but an absolute shortage has been avoided largely because the US, Europe and China released a good deal of their massive petroleum reserves, both from government Strategic Petroleum Reserves and from those of private companies. Reserves have fallen to unhealthy levels in the US, however, and if trade through Hormuz remains disrupted, European truckers may not be able to find diesel at gas stations at all this winter.
European countries belonging to the International Energy Agency had released nearly 300 million barrels of petroleum by mid-July, according to  Reuters. These countries had a billion barrels in reserve but are committed to releasing 40% of it to cover the current crisis. European SPRs are thus down by  30% already this year.  If there is a continued such shortfall in new diesel refining for the rest of this year, diesel prices could go through the roof as China and Europe bid on a dwindling supply. If Europe wants diesel, it will have to pay through the nose in a few months to keep it from all going instead to China.
 Photo by kai muro on Unsplash
The shortfall in diesel is mainly from lack of refining capacity.  The Gulf has a refining capacity for petroleum products of 11.7 million barrels a day. About a third of that is diesel and jet fuel. A big chunk isn’t being exported because of the staccato blockade of the Strait. That capacity has been further reduced because Ukraine has begun targeting Russian refineries with drones. Russia has responded with a diesel export ban. These developments have a global impact, since taking Russian diesel off the world market impels countries like India to source it elsewhere, competing with other importers.
It isn’t just Europe that faces a problem.  Kenya may lose $800 million because of higher petroleum prices by the end of the year if the crisis continues.
Meanwhile,  India is paying a whopping $25 million charter fee to bring Iraqi petroleum out of the Gulf.

35,2026-08-09,"Hyperliquid has never traded more contracts, and it has never kept a smaller share of the money those contracts earn
Open interest, the total value of leveraged positions traders hold at one time, climbed to just above $11 billion on July 13, the platform's highest in 2026. Hyperliquid’s perpetual futures volume over the past 30 days ran to nearly $178 billion. Hyperliquid now settles roughly 9% of all open perp positions worldwide, centralized exchanges included, up from under 7% in late May.
But the platform’s revenue has gone the other way. Gross protocol revenue peaked at roughly $357 million in the third quarter of 2025 and has fallen every quarter since, to nearly $295 million, then roughly $217 million, then about $202 million in the second quarter of 2026, DefiLlama data shows. That is a 43% drop from the top, booked while the trade count climbed.
Hyperliquid Improvement Proposal (HIP-3) helps explain why Hyperliquid is keeping less of the activity it attracts. Since October 2025, anyone who stakes 500,000 HYPE, worth about $28 million at current prices, can deploy their own perpetual futures market on Hyperliquid's order books and keep up to half the trading fees.
At the start of 2026 these builder-deployed markets were about 2% of Hyperliquid's perp volume. They are now roughly half of it.
The pass-through shows up in the accounts. Cost of revenue, the portion of fees Hyperliquid hands straight back to builders, market makers and its own liquidity vault, was under 6% of gross revenue in the second quarter of 2025. A year later it was 18%.
Builder code fees, which front-ends like Phantom charge on top for routing an order, arrived at roughly $16 million of revenue in the second quarter and left as roughly $16 million of cost in the same quarter. Every dollar of it passes through.
Traders keep showing up because of what those builder markets list. Real-world asset perps, contracts on things like crude oil, gold, Nvidia, Tesla, a Nasdaq-100 tracker and pre-IPO names like SpaceX, hit a record $3.6 billion in open interest this month and overtook bitcoin as the platform's largest market by that measure.
Between July 13 and July 19, tokenized stocks and commodities did $25 billion in volume, 52% of the weekly total, outpacing crypto perps for the first time. The contracts settle in stablecoins, never expire, and trade through the weekend when the New York Stock Exchange is shut. A product such as leveraged Nvidia exposure, at 2 a.m. on a Sunday, has few other homes.
That growth sits largely on one set of shoulders. Trade.xyz accounts for more than 90% of all HIP-3 open interest, which means Hyperliquid's record numbers depend on a single deployer's oracle choices, margin settings and risk management.
The risk in that arrangement showed earlier this week on Monday, when a single trade on a thin Korean pre-market venue dropped Trade.xyz's SK Hynix contract 19% and triggered liquidations the firm has since agreed to reimburse.
Hyperliquid routes about 97% of trading fees into its Assistance Fund, which buys the token on the open market and retires it, taking roughly 44.5 million HYPE out of the total supply so far. The buyback is a fixed share of earnings, so it contracts when earnings contract. The fund bought nearly $290 million of HYPE in the third quarter of 2025. In the second quarter of 2026 it bought roughly $149 million, close to half as much.
HYPE traded near $55 on Friday, down 5% on the week and roughly 28% below the June 16 record near $77, CoinDesk data show. Annualized earnings of about $785 million put the token at roughly 16 times its circulating market value and about 70 times fully diluted.
Institutional holders including Multicoin Capital and Bitwise have moved sizeable amounts of HYPE to exchanges over the past month.
The ecosystem around it is thinner than a top-15 ranking suggests. Of the 48 tokens CoinGecko tracks in the Hyperliquid category, HYPE accounts for almost all of the value. The next two, Ethena's USDe at about $4.5 billion and USDT0 at roughly $4 billion, are stablecoins issued elsewhere and bridged in. The largest natively built token is PURR at about $53 million, under half a percent of HYPE. The market still values HYPE largely on Hyperliquid’s exchange economics rather than a broad base of native applications.
Supply and regulators press from the other side. Nearly 10 million HYPE unlocked to core contributors on Aug. 6, about $550 million at current prices, one of a monthly series running through 2027 against a circulating supply of only 222 million.
Spot HYPE ETFs posted their first weekly outflow in the week to July 17, roughly $7 million, ending a nine-week inflow run. Singapore's MAS added the platform to its investor alert list in late June, following earlier U.K. warnings, and CME and ICE executives have pushed the CFTC to review its commodity perps.
Meanwhile, competition has arrived from an unexpected direction. Robinhood Chain, the brokerage's month-old network, has been clearing more than $600 million in daily decentralized-exchange volume on memecoin trading, and by some measures now draws more daily speculative activity than Hyperliquid does.
None of which is the same as saying the business is failing. ARK research put Hyperliquid and Pump.fun together at 67% of all crypto application revenue as of July 31, and Grayscale has compared the platform to Amazon Web Services, a place where outside developers build the products while the operator takes a cut of everything traded.
That comparison contains the problem. Hyperliquid booked roughly $45 million in gross revenue through the first four weeks of the third quarter. Hold that pace and the quarter lands near $150 million, a fourth straight decline, and the bid under HYPE thins with it.

36,2026-08-09,"On a gloomy July evening, Dean Ball is appraising the political theory shelf in the basement of San Francisco's City Lights Bookstore. It's the end of his second day at OpenAI, and he's arrived straight from orientation in a cashmere zip-up hoodie, carrying a company-issued tote bag. He pulls a copy of Friedrich Hayek's ""The Pure Theory of Capital"" from the shelf, notes how rare it is to see it in the wild, and flips through its pages. It's a fitting choice.
Ball has landed in a distinctly Hayekian role at OpenAI. The 20th century economist, as Ball recounts to me, argued that political change begins not at the ballot box but among intellectuals, whose ideas would eventually filter into government. His work inspired a generation of right-wing think tanks that helped remake American politics. One of them, the Manhattan Institute, gave Ball his first job after college.
In June, OpenAI announced that it had hired the 34-year-old Ball to lead its new ""strategic futures"" team, a think tank of sorts, charged with shaping AI policy from within the company. It's a broad mandate for a nervy time. Nearly everyone agrees artificial intelligence is becoming more powerful; many believe it could become catastrophically dangerous. In late July a rogue OpenAI agent hacked into the AI startup Hugging Face's systems on its own, sending a fresh wave of fears about the technology's capacities. There is little agreement over how, when, or by whom AI should be regulated.
If Ball — who's made a name for himself as a voluble Substack writer and as a key author of the Trump administration's AI Action Plan — gets his way, the AI labs will become what he calls a ""counterbalance to government."" They will continue to churn out increasingly powerful technology, unconstrained by laws that would ban development or preempt upheaval in the labor market. ""Dean has spent a lot of time thinking seriously about some of the biggest questions frontier AI labs need to get right,"" Ball's new boss, OpenAI strategy head Jason Kwon, said in a statement. ""He'll help us pressure-test our assumptions,"" Kwon added, and think through questions around AI safety, economic impacts, and the ""evolving relationship between governments, companies, and society."" Ball tells me he wants to articulate genuinely new visions for what superintelligent AI can deliver. He'll leave direct lobbying to others. ""I'm way too spiky of a guy for that,"" he says.
Spiky is right — Ball's rise from local policy wonk into AI's elite has earned him a fair share of enemies. Some critics say he's a naive AI booster, others that his AI-era William F. Buckley persona is a put-on. Emil Michael, who leads the Pentagon's research and engineering office, called Ball the ""supreme village idiot"" of AI in a July post to X, adding, ""Dean Ball has perhaps the biggest gap between actual IQ and his own perceived IQ of anyone in the industry (about 40 points)."" Michael isn't his only former colleague to disavow him. The White House declined to provide an on-the-record statement for this story, and connected me with a current official who asked to remain anonymous. The official tells me that Ball is no longer trusted by the administration and claims that he's dramatically inflated his impact on the action plan.
Ball says he still has friends in the administration and people across agencies that he advises every day. There are people he's pissed off, too, but the idea that he inflated his input on the action plan is ""completely false,"" he says. ""If you mean it, say it on the fucking record.""
I spoke with Ball a few times during his first weeks at OpenAI. A self-described ""information hedonist,"" he's equally delighted to dissect avant-garde film as influential policy research. Over a dinner in San Francisco, he pontificated about the Enlightenment, cited many of his own predictions, spoke in machine-learning analogies and Latin phrases, and said that parts of Biden-era AI policy were ""woke shit."" He's become an influential figure within AI's executive class, making him a useful weather vane for the technology's changing political winds.

Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
Ball was raised middle-class in Northern Virginia, by parents with what he called ""lib-coded"" jobs. His dad, a punk rock drummer turned therapist, would take him on ride-alongs to clients struggling with substance abuse. ""I saw a lot of shit when I was young. He exposed me to that stuff,"" he says. Finishing high school in 2010, Ball still felt ""apathetic"" about politics.
He arrived at Hamilton College, the upstate New York liberal arts school, to find that the professor he'd adored in a pre-enrollment lecture had been denied tenure and had left for the Alexander Hamilton Institute, a conservative think tank nearby. Ball visited, joined a Hayek reading group, and was bowled over. The idea that he wasn't being handed the economist's work in his regular college classes felt ludicrous.
""I kind of just realized, wait, fighting in the battle of ideas is totally what I want to be doing,"" Ball says. ""Because this is ridiculous. This guy is so much smarter than any of my professors and the fact that my professors would deign to tell me I shouldn't read him for whatever stupid, narrow-minded reason they have, I cannot abide by that."" He started inhaling right-of-center political theory.
After graduating, he joined the Manhattan Institute, focusing on city and state policy. Vanessa Mendoza, his boss at the time, says that when she met Ball, he stuck out as an ""immediate adult."" He would come early and stay late to hear from scholars outside his remit, and went on to work at think tanks for over a decade.
After ChatGPT's release at the end of 2022, Ball began to worry that conservatives weren't seriously considering how powerful AI could become. If a guy with a nanotechnology fabricator destroys the galaxy, he quips, Americans will probably lose some individual liberties. He eventually decided to enter the fray, launching his Substack, ""Hyperdimensional,"" at the beginning of 2024. He began with a spritely free-market screed, declaring that he would write from a ""classical liberal and un-apologetically pro-progress perspective."" The private sector had ""grown up""; its firms wielded capital that ""would make Stalin and the rest of the 20th century's foremost central planners blush.""
Ball writes in a high-minded first person. He deploys ""fundamentally,"" ""necessarily,"" and ""profoundly"" often for emphasis. He profoundly loves nautical metaphors. America's founding ideals are ""our sole ballasts against the gusts""; a White House staffer's work requires cutting through the ""hull of a gargantuan ship"" in search of the engine room. AI policy, as of July 2024, was an oceanic abyss: ""You can try to cast an anchor into the bottomless sea, but the ship will just start sinking, inch by inch.""
He started making his name among AI's elite later that year, when he leaped into the fight over a proposed bill in California that would have created a board to regulate AI models and mandated safety protocols for AI labs. Even the safety-minded researchers whom he opposed were impressed with Ball's thoughtfulness, which stood out amid bad-faith takes from the anti-regulation side. He wasn't ""caricaturing or being deceptive"" but engaging with the actual text, Helen Toner, an OpenAI board member at the time, tells me. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said on a podcast that Ball's ""scholarly"" writing helped him understand the bill, which Gov. Gavin Newsom eventually vetoed.
Then Trump won the 2024 election. Ball didn't yet think he'd built the reputation to win a job, but suddenly heard from a member of the transition team.
During the first week of his new term, Trump signed an executive order on AI, calling for his administration to draft a pro-business policy that would ""sustain and enhance America's global AI dominance in order to promote human flourishing, economic competitiveness, and national security.""
Ball joined a team led by Michael Kratsios, Trump's policy director for science and technology, and David Sacks, his AI and crypto advisor, and the task soon took over his life. Ball says he ""held the pen"" on this policy document, writing the 20-plus-page first draft himself and, over the coming months, crafting several new versions with feedback from the leaders and the dozens of federal agencies that the plan's proposals would affect.
The current White House official contested this account, saying Ball had little impact on the AI Action Plan's policy process or wording. Kratsios and Sacks had sign-off on the plan, and dozens of staffers contributed, the official added. Ball says it's ""just not accurate"" that his role was minor.
The administration published the plan in July, with recommendations for countering Chinese influence and convening meetings about evaluating AI, amid dozens of other ideas. It was effectively a strategy document; none of it is binding, and many of the recommendations remain incomplete.
Ball left that August, after less than five months at the White House, and before obtaining a security clearance. He tells me the work was done: He'd joined to write the action plan, and then written it. He says he realized he'd be more effective as a writer and commentator from the outside — especially since he felt the administration was downplaying AI's progress.
He began a new phase as an AI talking head, as his audience continued to grow on X and Substack, and he was repeatedly quoted in the press as a ""former White House official."" In February, when the Department of Defense designated Anthropic as a national security supply chain risk over a contract dispute, Ball tore into his old employer. ""Our public institutions behaved erratically, maliciously, and without strategic clarity,"" he wrote. ""Our political leaders conveyed little understanding of their own actions, to say nothing of the technology and its stakes.""
Another spat opened up between Anthropic and the administration in April, and Ball again criticized the government's response. Ball had gone to work inside the ""belly of the beast,"" as Max Bodach, a friend of Ball's and former think tank colleague, tells me, but came out with his same, classic, libertarian distrust of the state. Bodach said he thinks Ball has won more and more respect because ""he really prizes his independence of thought above everything else in life.""
Anthropic's contentious relationship with the White House — Trump called its leaders ""left-wing nut jobs"" at one point — has given many in the AI world the impression that OpenAI is the cannier political player. The White House official shared this view, but added that given the administration's current stance on Ball, they're surprised OpenAI hired him.
Despite his distaste for state intervention, Ball does believe the time is right for some regulation — or, as he put it on X, ""moderate prudence beyond what the incentives of advanced capitalism on its own might provide for."" As AI models advance, an industry consensus is emerging on the need for mandatory safety testing. Anthropic's Amodei, Google DeepMind cofounder Demis Hassabis, and OpenAI have each recently suggested ideas for government-backed safety audits.
Ball has voiced support for versions of this idea for over a year — he particularly liked the AI nonprofit METR's recent holistic audit of the labs, in which the auditors collaborated with the industry while reckoning with the current dangers of the tech.
Over the coming months, Ball says, he hopes to build a stronger consensus on what the safety audit structure might actually look like. ""We need a common communal sense of like what these auditors are going to do exactly,"" he says. ""What is in scope? What is out of scope? What is the point of them?""
OpenAI's global affairs team, the group more directly responsible for lobbying, takes the lead on regulatory matters in DC and at state capitols. That team has drawn critics, in part due to its leader, Chris Lehane, who helped advise the pro-AI, anti-regulation super PAC Leading the Future.
Nathan Calvin, an AI policy researcher who opposed Ball in the California bill fight but has since come to see him as a friend, said he hopes Ball can act as a counterweight to the global affairs team. Ball, Calvin says, could be ""someone who is trying to make sure that the policies that OpenAI is putting forward are of the moment, and taking things seriously.""
Ball says his team's long-term remit will be reckoning with the evolving questions about superintelligent AI and the relationships between labs and government along the way. So far, the new hire has mostly given OpenAI unwelcome publicity. After the July release of a new open-weight model by the Chinese lab Moonshot AI, Ball published a 660-word X post, writing that a world where open-weight models — which allow anyone to download and tweak the tech — are dominant could end with ""full AI communism."" He also suggested the models deter frontier AI research spending, and that the Trump administration might realize their ""best strategy here"" would be to cloak Chinese models in regulatory risk.
A wave of vitriol followed, including the Pentagon staffer's ""village idiot"" comment and a barb from Sacks, as Ball's post soared past 10 million views. A narrative emerged that Ball was just talking his book as an OpenAI employee.
Before Ball took the OpenAI job, he'd secured a guarantee in his contract that he'd still be able to write independently on X and his Substack. (TBPN, the chummy tech news streaming show OpenAI acquired in April, has similarly said that it remains editorially independent.) But the response was a bitter lesson.
""It is effectively impossible to do the kind of writing I used to do on this website, not because anyone at OpenAI censors me but because of the sheer volume of hostility I get for sharing my analysis as a frontier lab employee,"" Ball wrote on X.
This fits a prediction I heard from David Krueger, an AI safety researcher who thinks Ball doesn't take the idea of powerful superintelligence seriously enough. Krueger says that he hopes Ball's position at OpenAI will detract from his credibility. ""When you work for an industry, people are more skeptical about what you're saying, and are more likely to ask, 'Is this person a shill?'"" he says. ""And I think that's healthy and good.""
Alex Turner, an AI safety researcher who recently left Google DeepMind, says that while he's appreciated Ball's thoughtfulness in the past, ""I feel like usually when people go to a lab, they become much less able to think clearly about what their group is doing, what their lab is doing. I'd hate to see that happen to Dean.""
Ball tells me that his opinions haven't changed much since his start at OpenAI, and that if anything, he's more pro-regulatory. But he's realizing he's now seen differently. The first month on the job has been a tricky one for AI policy's most prominent critic — just as the stakes get higher and the politics get gnarlier.
""My job relies on me maintaining some degree of credibility, and ability to speak to a diverse array of people and not just a sort of predictable set of allies, right?"" Ball told me a week before his open-source post. ""That's the heart of what I do for a living. If I'm in a world where I can't do that, it's a real problem.""
Stephen Council is a senior tech reporter at Business Insider, covering OpenAI, Anthropic, and the ecosystem around the leading artificial intelligence companies.
